                                                              Recovery of Planted Subgraphs
                                                                                Wasim Huleihel
                                                                                   July 2, 2026
arXiv:2607.00897v1 [cs.IT] 1 Jul 2026




                                                                                      Abstract
                                                  Understanding the fundamental limits of recovering planted subgraphs in random
                                              graphs is a central challenge in high-dimensional statistics and theoretical computer
                                              science. While existing work has largely focused on special subgraph families such as
                                              cliques, bicliques, or dense blocks, the exact recovery of a general planted subgraph in
                                              Erdős–Rényi random graphs remains poorly understood. In this paper, we study the
                                              exact recovery of an arbitrary planted subgraph Γ = Γn embedded in a dense Erdős–
                                              Rényi random graph G(n, qn ), where edges within Γ are present independently with
                                              probability pn > qn .
                                                  Our main results identify sharp conditions under which exact recovery is possible
                                              with high probability, and we establish matching lower bounds showing the neces-
                                              sity of these conditions. The resulting statistical threshold is characterized by a new
                                              graph-theoretic quantity, which we term the minimal maximum subgraph density. This
                                              quantity is defined as the maximum subgraph density of the smallest induced balanced
                                              subgraph of Γ.
                                                  We then turn to the problem of recovery under polynomial-time constraints. We
                                              propose a computationally efficient recovery algorithm that applies to arbitrary planted
                                              subgraphs and analyze its performance in terms of certain spectral properties of the ad-
                                              jacency matrix. In addition, we derive computational lower bounds for recovery using
                                              the low-degree polynomial framework, establishing regimes where recovery is statisti-
                                              cally possible but computationally hard. Finally, we consider several extensions of our
                                              setting, including recovery in semi-random models and weaker notions of recovery.




                                            W. Huleihel is with the School of Electrical Engineering and Computer Engineering, at Tel Aviv Uni-
                                        versity, Tel Aviv 6997801, Israel (e-mail: wasimh@tauex.tau.ac.il).

                                                2
1    Introduction
The study of structured signals in networks lies at the intersection of graph theory, com-
puter science, and statistics, with applications ranging from social networks to computational
biology. A central question in this area is whether one can reliably reconstruct hidden or
anomalous structures embedded in otherwise random graphs. Much of the existing literature
has focused on identifying communities or clusters of vertices with unusually high internal
connectivity. While early work emphasized detection, namely determining whether such a
structure is present, an equally fundamental and arguably more challenging task is recovery:
given an observed network, can one pinpoint the precise location of the hidden structure?
This recovery perspective is crucial in applications such as anomaly localization, motif dis-
covery in biological networks, and targeted monitoring in social networks, and it raises new
theoretical questions regarding the statistical and computational limits of inference.
     As in many inference problems, recovery exhibits both statistical and computational
facets. The statistical aspect concerns the feasibility of recovery given unlimited computa-
tional power, whereas the computational aspect asks whether recovery can be achieved effi-
ciently. Traditionally, these aspects were studied separately, with information-theoretic tools
providing fundamental limits. However, recent work has highlighted the critical role of com-
putational constraints in high-dimensional inference. A growing body of research (see, e.g.,
[BR13, MW15b, CLR17, CX16, HS17, HB18, GJW20, BHK+ 16, ZK16, LKZ15, HWX15a,
BPW18, BBH18, BBH19, BB20], among many others) has identified striking statistical–
computational gaps in planted combinatorial problems, that is, regimes where recovery is
information-theoretically possible but no known polynomial-time algorithm succeeds.
     In this paper, we investigate the problem of recovering an arbitrary subgraph planted
in an Erdős–Rényi random graph. Formally, let n ∈ N, let pn ∈ (0, 1) and qn ∈ (0, 1) satisfy
qn < pn ≤ 1, and let Γ = Γn denote an arbitrary sequence of undirected graphs, referred to
as the planted subgraph. We observe a graph G generated as follows: first, a copy Γ⋆n of Γn
is chosen uniformly at random among all embeddings in the complete graph on n vertices;
edges within Γ⋆n are then included independently with probability pn , while all remaining
edges are included independently with probability qn . The inferential task is to recover the
precise location of Γ⋆n , namely to achieve exact recovery, with high probability.
     Over the past several decades, numerous special cases of planted subgraph recovery
have been studied. The canonical example is the planted clique problem [Jer92], where the
goal is to recover a hidden k-clique embedded in G(n, 1/2). Other notable examples include
the planted dense subgraph problem [ACV14, ACBL15, HWX15a], the recovery of planted
trees [MST19], Hamiltonian cycles [BDT+ 20], matchings [MMX21], and bipartite structures
[RHS24]. Despite shared methodological features, the statistical and computational behav-
iors vary significantly across different planted structures. Some subgraphs exhibit sharp
recovery thresholds and conjectured computationally hard regimes, as in the planted clique
problem, whereas others, such as paths or stars, appear not to exhibit any computational
barrier [MST19].
     There has been recent progress toward unified frameworks for planted subgraph infer-
ence. For the detection problem, where the goal is to distinguish between a pure Erdős–Rényi
random graph and the planted model described above, [ABBDL10, Hul22, YZZ24, EH25,
EH26] studied general detection models for arbitrary planted subgraphs in various settings

                                              3
and established both statistical and computational thresholds, primarily in the dense regime.
Collectively, these results provide a nearly complete understanding of detection: when it is
statistically possible and when it can be achieved efficiently.
     By contrast, the landscape for the recovery problem remains far less understood. Impor-
tant progress was made in [MNWS+ 23, LPRZ25], which studied weak recovery for arbitrary
planted subgraphs and derived a general variational formula for the limiting minimum mean
squared error (MMSE) under mild structural density assumptions. These results yield a
powerful statistical characterization of recovery in the weak sense. Nevertheless, several key
questions remain open. First, the focus there is on approximate or fractional recovery, as
captured by small MMSE, rather than on exact recovery or other natural notions of re-
covery. Different recovery criteria can lead to qualitatively different thresholds, governed
by fundamentally different graph-theoretic measures. Second, while these works character-
ize statistical limits, they do not address computational tractability: general computational
lower and upper bounds for recovery are still missing. As a result, it remains unclear when
a statistical–computational gap arises and how efficient recovery can be achieved.
     In this paper, we take a step toward addressing these challenges by developing a general
framework for recovery in the arbitrary planted subgraph setting. Our approach simultane-
ously addresses statistical and computational aspects and accommodates multiple notions of
recovery. Motivated by the gaps described above, we pose the following guiding questions:
       What graph-theoretic properties of Γ govern the statistical and computational
                                    limits of recovery?
        For which planted structures does a statistical–computational gap emerge?


1.1    Main contributions
In this paper, we aim to answer the questions posed above. We begin with what is arguably
the most canonical setting of the problem: the dense regime, where the edge probabilities
(pn , qn ) are fixed constants. Our first objective is to characterize the statistical (information-
theoretic) limits of exact recovery of the planted subgraph Γ = Γn , as defined above (see
Section 2 for formal details).
       A key ingredient in our analysis is the onion decomposition of Γ = Γn , a concept re-
cently introduced in [LPRZ25]. Informally, this decomposition iteratively peels off subgraphs
achieving the maximum subgraph density of Γ (see Definition 1), layer by layer, until the
entire graph is decomposed into the union of such layers. A precise definition is given in
Definition 4. Our results are expressed in terms of a graph-theoretic quantity that we call
the minimal maximum subgraph density, defined as

                                 µmin (Γn ) ≜ min max η(F|S).
                                              S⊆Γn S⊊F⊆Γn


Here, η(·|·) denotes the relative density, as defined in Definition 3. This quantity admits a
natural operational interpretation: it coincides with the maximum subgraph density of the
final, and hence minimal, layer in the onion decomposition of Γ.
      Our first main result shows that the statistical threshold for exact recovery is governed by



                                                 4
                                             Possible           log n
                               µmin (Γn )      ≷         C                  ,
                                            Impossible       dKL (pn ||qn )

where dKL (pn ||qn ) denotes the Kullback–Leibler divergence between Bern(pn ) and Bern(qn ),
and C ≥ 1 is a universal constant. In particular, if the minimal maximum subgraph density
of Γ exceeds the right-hand side, then exact recovery is achievable with high probability;
otherwise, exact recovery is information-theoretically impossible. The achievability result
is obtained by applying a maximum-likelihood estimator (MLE) layer by layer along the
onion decomposition, while the converse follows from a genie-aided argument combined with
Fano’s inequality. Together, these results fully characterize the statistical limits of exact
planted subgraph recovery in the dense regime.
      We next turn to the computational aspect of the problem. We propose a general,
computationally efficient recovery algorithm and analyze its performance guarantees. This
method can be viewed as a semidefinite relaxation of the optimal MLE. We show that it
succeeds whenever certain spectral properties of an appropriate transformation of the adja-
cency matrix of Γn , specifically its rank and coherence number, satisfy suitable conditions.
Notably, the resulting algorithm and its guarantees recover, as special cases, the best-known
polynomial-time procedures for classical planted subgraph problems, including cliques, bi-
partite graphs, and related models. For certain choices of Γ, however, there remains a
gap between the information-theoretic limits established above and the performance of this
efficient algorithm. We conjecture that this gap is intrinsic, in the sense that below the
corresponding computational threshold, no polynomial-time algorithm can achieve recovery.
      To provide evidence for this conjecture, we adopt the low-degree polynomial framework
of [SW22] and establish computational lower bounds for recovery. Roughly speaking, we show
that all polynomials of degree O(log n) fail to recover Γ (even in a weak sense) whenever its
density satisfies

                                                |e(Γn )|  √
                                  η(Γn ) ≜               ≪ n.
                                                |v(Γn )|
For instance,
            √ when Γn is a clique, this condition predicts computational hardness whenever
|v(Γn )| ≪ n. Similarly, for a complete bipartite graph with kL left and k√ R right vertices,
recovery is conjectured to be computationally hard when min{kL , kR } ≪ n. These pre-
dictions align with well-known folklore conjectures. We also establish complementary
                                                                            √         upper
bounds, showing that low-degree polynomials succeed whenever η(Γn ) ≫ n for a broad
class of nearly regular subgraphs, including cliques, balanced bipartite graphs, and sparse
expander graphs. In these cases, the low-degree predictions match the performance of the
best known polynomial-time algorithms.
     Finally, we consider several extensions of the baseline planted subgraph model. The
standard formulation assumes a purely random generative process, which may be fragile
under adversarial perturbations. To address this issue, we study semi-random models in
which an adversary may delete edges outside the planted subgraph and add edges inside
it prior to observation. Importantly, the statistician does not know which edges have been
modified. For this setting, known as the monotone adversary model [FK00, FK01], we show
that both the optimal MLE and the efficient algorithm proposed above remain robust and

                                                    5
achieve the same recovery guarantees as in the non-adversarial model.
     Motivated by the observation that exact recovery is fundamentally impossible for planted
subgraphs consisting of a dense core with a very sparse appendage, such as kite graphs, since
their minimal maximum subgraph density µmin is sub-logarithmic in the number of vertices,
we also study weaker notions of recovery. Specifically, we consider exact layer recovery,
where the goal is to recover a fixed number of initial layers in the onion decomposition,
as well as almost-exact recovery [HWX17, WX20], where the estimator is required to be
close to the planted subgraph in Hamming distance. For both recovery criteria, we establish
asymptotically tight thresholds.

1.2    Related work
This paper lies within a broad literature on planted combinatorial structure in random graphs
and matrices, studied from both statistical and computational perspectives. We highlight
work most pertinent to recovery; for a fuller survey, see [EH25].
Planted subgraphs and matrices. One of the earliest and most influential recovery
problems is the planted clique. Alon, Krivelevich, and √ Sudakov [AKS98] showed that
spectral methods can identify a clique of size k = Ω( n). Since then, a variety of algo-
rithmic approaches have been studied—including combinatorial heuristics [FK00, McS01,
FR10], convex relaxations such as semidefinite programming and nuclear-norm minimiza-
tion [AV11, DM15a], and approximate message passing√ [CX16]. Despite these advances, all
known polynomial-time algorithms require k = Ω( n), giving rise to the widely accepted
planted√clique conjecture, which asserts that recovery is computationally intractable when
k = o( n). Beyond cliques, researchers have explored other planted subgraph models:
• Independent sets. Feige and Krauthgamer [FO05] proposed a spectral method for recov-
  ering planted independent sets, while Coja-Oghlan [CO03] established polynomial-time
  recovery guarantees in sparse regimes where q = Θ(n−α ), provided the independence
  number scales appropriately. Additional work has analyzed the limits of greedy and local
  algorithms in these settings [COE15, GS14, RV+ 17].
• Dense subgraphs and communities. The problem of recovering a dense community em-
  bedded in an Erdős–Rényi background has attracted extensive attention [ACV14, BI13,
  VAC15, HWX15b, Mon15, CC18, HWX17, CX16]. A key milestone was the reduction
  from planted clique to planted dense subgraph, due to Hajek, Wu, and Xu [HWX15a],
  which established hardness in regimes where p = cq and q = Θ(n−α ). Brennan et al.
  [BBH18] later extended this reduction to nearly the full range of p > q, with transitions
  to denser regimes analyzed in [BCC+ 10].
• Other planted structures. A variety of recovery problems have been studied for specific
  combinatorial templates, including: planted trees [MST19], Hamiltonian cycles [BDT+ 20],
  perfect matchings [MMX21], bipartite subgraphs [RHS24], and cycles [CM24, MWZ23].
  These studies demonstrate that the algorithmic and statistical behavior can differ drasti-
  cally depending on the underlying subgraph: some exhibit sharp thresholds and conjec-
  tured computational barriers, while others allow efficient algorithms down to information-
  theoretic limits.

                                             6
• Matrices and Gaussian models. Beyond graphs, analogous recovery problems appear in
  high-dimensional statistics. A prime example is Gaussian biclustering, where one seeks
  to identify a planted submatrix with elevated mean. Detection aspects were analyzed in
  [BI13, MW15b, MRZ15], while recovery guarantees were developed in [SWP+ 09, KBRS11,
  BKR+ 11, CLR17, CX16, HWX17, BBH19, DHB24b, DHB24a]. Closely related are spec-
  tral analyses of the spiked Wigner model, beginning with [Péc06, FP07, CDMF+ 09], and
  later work on spectral thresholds and Bayesian algorithms [MRZ15, PWB20, PWBM18,
  BMV+ 18, HKP+ 17].

Together, this body of research illustrates the diversity of recovery phenomena: in some cases
(e.g., planted cliques and dense subgraphs), computational-statistical gaps appear central,
while in others (e.g., certain trees or paths), efficient recovery is achievable down to statistical
thresholds.
General planting models. Research has long studied planting arbitrary substructures in
random graphs, exploring both detection and recovery under varied generative models. Early
works like [ABBDL10] developed general probabilistic bounds for testing in the planted-
vector setting—though their results were broad and not always tight across all cases. More
recent lines of inquiry have introduced two foundational paradigms:

1. Detection. Several works have developed frameworks for the detection of arbitrary planted
   subgraphs. The work [Hul22] introduced two models for planting arbitrary subgraphs in
   random graphs: the union model (where the planted edges are superimposed on an Erdős–
   Rényi background) and the induced model (where the planted subgraph appears as an
   induced copy). They analyzed detection thresholds in the dense regime, providing both
   information-theoretic bounds and computational insights. Recently, [YZZ24] studied the
   computational limits of detection in the dense regime and showed that optimal constant-
   degree polynomial tests are always given by counting stars. This result highlights the
   limitations of low-degree polynomials for detection of arbitrary planted subgraphs. Re-
   cently, [EH25] extended this line of work by analyzing detection in the union model more
   generally. Their results provide sharp characterizations across both sparse and dense
   regimes, demonstrating how the feasibility of detection depends delicately on graph den-
   sity and subgraph structure. Together, these works clarify the statistical and algorithmic
   landscape for detection in general planted subgraph models, identifying both tractable
   and hard regimes. Finally, [EH26] established fundamental statistical limits for detecting
   arbitrary planted subgraph under a semi-random model where an adversary is allowed to
   remove edges outside the planted subgraph before the graph is provided to the statistician;
   the goal is to derive robust detection algorithms.

2. Recovery. The problem of recovery, where the goal is to reconstruct the precise location
   of the planted subgraph, has also been addressed in several recent studies. In [Hul22]
   considered recovery in the induced model, deriving bounds in the dense regime. While
   their focus was primarily on detection, they established statistical thresholds for recovery
   as well. In [MNWS+ 23] investigated recovery for arbitrary planted subgraphs in the dense
   regime. They provided tight upper and lower bounds for recovery in this setting, focusing
   on specific families of subgraphs, and highlighted where statistical–computational gaps


                                                 7
   arise. Finally, [LPRZ25] advanced this direction by deriving an exact formula for the
   asymptotic MMSE curve for recovering arbitrary planted subgraphs. They also proposed
   an efficient algorithm for approximating this threshold in dense graphs, based on a novel
   decomposition technique, extending “all-or-nothing” phenomena to general subgraphs.
   Taken together, these works establish the first general frameworks for recovery in arbitrary
   planted subgraph models, with [MNWS+ 23] and [LPRZ25] providing sharp results in the
   dense setting, and [Hul22] bridging induced subgraphs and recovery.

Computational hardness. Over the past decade, major progress has been made to-
ward a rigorous understanding of the fundamental limits of efficient algorithms for high-
dimensional inference problems with planted structure. A recurring theme in this line of
work is the emergence of a statistical–computational gap: the number of samples required by
any known polynomial-time algorithm is strictly larger than the information-theoretic mini-
mum [BR13, MW15b, CLR17, KNV15, HWX15a, CX16, WBP16, WBS16, GMZ17, BBH18,
BBH19, WX20, BB20, HS17, HB18, BKW20, CHK+ 20, GJW20, BHK+ 16, DM15b, MPW15,
MW15a, KMOW17, HKP+ 18, RSS19, HKP+ 17, MRX20, FGR+ 17, FPV15, DKS17, DKS19,
ZK16, LKZ15, LdBB+ 16, KMRT+ 07, RTSZ19, BPW18, SW22, BBH+ 21, Wei21, AD23,
EH25]. The evidence for these gaps typically falls into two broad categories:

1. Failure of classes of algorithms. One line of evidence comes from showing that broad
   algorithmic paradigms fail in the conjectured hard regime. For instance, low-degree poly-
   nomials provide a unifying lens for analyzing high-dimensional inference, and their failure
   below certain thresholds suggests sharp computational barriers [HS17, HB18, BKW20,
   CHK+ 20, GJW20]. Similarly, the sum-of-squares hierarchy, despite its power as a fam-
   ily of semidefinite relaxations, has been shown to fall short in planted clique, planted
   dense subgraph, and related problems [BHK+ 16, DM15b, MPW15, MW15a, KMOW17,
   HKP+ 18, RSS19, HKP+ 17, MRX20]. The statistical query model, which captures a wide
   class of algorithms accessing only expectations of data-dependent functions, has also
   yielded strong lower bounds in this context [FGR+ 17, FPV15, DKS17, DKS19, BBH+ 21].
   Finally, message-passing algorithms such as belief propagation and approximate message
   passing often achieve optimal performance in “easy” regimes but exhibit provable failures
   in the hard phase across multiple planted models [ZK16, LKZ15, LdBB+ 16, KMRT+ 07,
   RTSZ19, BPW18].

2. Average-case reductions. Another powerful approach is to establish hardness by reducing
   one planted problem to another conjectured-to-be-hard task, most prominently planted
   clique. This technique has been used to show that recovery (or even detection) in models
   such as planted dense subgraph or biclustering is at least as hard as planted clique in
   certain parameter regimes [BR13, MW15b, CLR17, CX16, HWX15a, WBP16, WBS16,
   GMZ17, BBH18, BBH19, WX20, BB20].

Semi-random models. The notion of robustness via semi-random perturbations dates
back to Blum and Spencer [BS95], with stronger adversarial variants introduced in the
semi-random planted clique model [FK00, FK01]. These ideas have since been generalized
to community detection in the SBM [CSV17, MPW16, BMR21, BJK+ 24], and to recov-
ery of cliques [Ste17, MMT20, BKS23, BBKS24, GW25], bipartite graphs [KLP22a], and

                                              8
r-colorable graphs [LPR25]. While convex relaxations such as SDP often remain robust,
spectral and other classical methods can fail under semi-random noise. In the planted dense
subgraph model, Brennan et al. [BB20] conjectured that below certain density thresholds
even weak recovery is computationally hard with adversarial edge deletions, supporting their
claim via reductions from the planted clique with side information.

      We use standard graph-theoretic notation. An undirected graph is denoted by G =
(v(G), e(G)), with vertex set v(G) and edge set e(G). Since we primarily consider graphs
without isolated vertices, for a subgraph H ⊆ G we write |H| to denote the number of edges
in e(H). If |v(G)| ≤ n, we let SG denote the set of all isomorphic copies of G in the complete
graph Kn . That is, H ⊆ Kn belongs to SG if there exists a bijection f : v(G) → v(H) such
that (u, v) ∈ e(G) if and only if (f (u), f (v)) ∈ e(H). Given Γ ⊆ Kn and a subgraph H ⊆ Γ,
we define N (H, Γ) as the set of copies of H in Γ, and M(H, Γ) as the set of copies of Γ in Kn
that contain H. For any graph G, AG denotes its adjacency matrix, indexed by v(G), where
AG (i, j) = 1{{i, j} ∈ e(G)}.
      The rest of this paper is organized as follows. In Section 2, we introduce the problem
setup and provide some necessary preliminaries. Section 3 presents our main results, discus-
sions, and examples. Sections 4–6 are devoted to the the derivation of our lower and upper
bounds. Finally, in Section 7 we conclude our paper, and discuss a few directions for future
research.




                                                   9
2     Problem Setup and Preliminaries
In this section, we describe the setting we study, along with several important preliminaries.
Let Γ = (Γn )n∈N be a sequence of graphs such that, for each n ∈ N, Γn = (v(Γn ), e(Γn ))
is an undirected graph without isolated vertices and with |v(Γn )| ≤ n. Let SΓn denote the
set of all isomorphic copies of Γn in the complete graph on n vertices. We refer to Γn as
the planted (or hidden) structure. Fix parameters pn , qn satisfying 0 < qn < pn ≤ 1. The
planted subgraph model GΓn (n, pn , qn ) is defined as the distribution of a random graph G on
n vertices generated as follows: first draw an arbitrary but fixed copy Γ⋆n ∈ SΓn ; then include
each edge e ∈ e(Γ⋆n ) independently with probability pn , and include each edge e ∈       / e(Γ⋆n )
independently with probability qn . Equivalently, G can be viewed as the union of a noisy
copy of Γ⋆n and an Erdős–Rényi random graph G(n, qn ).
      A learner observes a single sample G ∼ GΓn (n, pn , qn ), and the goal is to recover the
hidden copy Γ⋆n . We study this framework in the asymptotic regime where n → ∞. Figure 1
illustrates typical graph observations. Given G, an estimator Γ     b : {0, 1}(n2 ) → SΓn aims to
output Γ⋆n . Define the worst-case error probability associated with an estimator Γ    b as

                            En (Γ̂) ≜ sup PGΓ⋆n (n,pn ,qn ) [Γ̂(G) ̸= Γ⋆ ],                    (1)
                                          Γ⋆ ∈SΓn

and the optimal error probability as
                       E⋆n ≜        inf             sup PGΓ⋆n (n,pn ,qn ) [Γ̂(G) ̸= Γ⋆ ].      (2)
                                        (2)
                               Γ̂:{0,1}n →SΓn Γ⋆ ∈SΓ


We say that a sequence of estimators (Γ                bn : {0, 1}(n2 ) → SΓn , achieves exact
                                       bn )n∈N , where Γ

                           bn ) = 0. Conversely, we say that exact recovery is impossible if
              ⋆

Remark 1. In the above, we focused on a worst-case definition of error probability. However,
as we show in Appendix A.1, the Bayesian (average-case) definition, where an expectation
over Γ⋆ ∼ Unif(SΓn ) is taken instead of a supremum, is equivalent. Indeed, the uniform mea-
sure over SΓn induces a permutation-invariant statistical model for which the least favorable
prior is also uniform.
     Our results will be expressed in terms of the following graph-theoretic measures. We
let η (Γn ) ≜ |e(Γn )|/|v(Γn )| denote the density of Γn , and we recall the following definition
of the maximum subgraph density.
Definition 1 (Maximum subgraph density [Bol01]). Let G be an undirected graph. The
maximum subgraph density of G is
                               µ(G) ≜ max {η(H) : H ⊆ G, H ̸= ∅} .                             (3)
     Next, we introduce the notion of a graph-cut.
Definition 2 (Graph-cut). Let n be a positive integer. A graph-cut on n vertices is a triplet
H = (V, S, E), where S ⊆ V ⊆ [n], and
           E ⊆ KV \ KS ≜ {(u, v) : u, v ∈ V and at most one of u, v belongs to S} .            (4)

                                                        10
            (a) Planted Γ⋆n = KkL ,kR .                               (b) Planted Γ⋆n = Pk

Figure 1: The observed graph G is the union of an Erdős–Rényi graph and a planted subgraph
Γ⋆n . In (a) Γ⋆n is a bipartite subgraph, and in (b) Γ⋆n is a path.


We define the number of edges of the graph-cut as |H| ≜ |E|, and the number of non-selected
vertices as |v(H)| ≜ |V \ S|.
     We note that throughout the paper, we use H to denote either a graph (identified with
its edge set) or a graph-cut, depending on the context. When H is a graph, v(H) denotes its
vertex set; when H = (V, S, E) is a graph-cut, v(H) denotes the set of non-selected vertices
V \S. This distinction will be clear from the context. An important graph-theoretic quantity
that will play a central role is the relative density and the maximum subgraph relative density,
defined as follows.
Definition 3 (Relative densities). Given graphs H′ ⊆ H (viewed as edge sets), we define the
induced graph-cut by H|H′ ≜ (v(H), v(H′ ), H \ H′ ). The relative density is defined as
                                                    |H| − |H′ |
                                     η(H|H′ ) ≜                   .                          (5)
                                                  |v(H) \ v(H′ )|

If H \ H′ = ∅, we define η(H|H′ ) ≜ ∞. The maximum subgraph relative density is
                             µ(H|H′ ) ≜ max {η(J|H′ ) : H′ ⊊ J ⊆ H} .                        (6)
     Note that, given H′ , we choose J ⊋ H′ in (6) to maximize the “cut density”, namely the
number of new edges per new vertex, counting also edges from the new vertices back into H′ .
This corresponds to the densest subgraph in the cut, rather than in the induced remainder.
For example, let H′ = K4 be a clique on 4 vertices (with 6 edges). Add many leaf vertices,
each joined to K4 by exactly one edge and with no edges among the leaves, and denote the
resulting graph by H. Then every added vertex contributes exactly one cross-edge to H′ , and
hence µ(H|H′ ) = 1.
     Next, our statistical lower and upper bounds will rely, both in the statements and in
the proofs, on a canonical decomposition of the planted subgraph Γ, introduced in [LPRZ25,
Definition 3.3].

                                                  11
Definition 4 (Onion decomposition). Let Γ = Γn be an arbitrary graph. The onion decom-
position of Γ is an increasing sequence of subgraphs Γ(0) , Γ(1) , . . . constructed as follows:
  (i) Initialize with Γ(0) ≜ ∅.
 (ii) For each ℓ > 0, let Γ(ℓ) be a maximal subgraph that maximizes η(H|Γ(ℓ−1) ) among all H
      such that Γ(ℓ−1) ⊊ H.1
(iii) Stop if Γ(ℓ) = Γ.
Let M = M (Γ) ≤ |Γ| denote the total number of steps until termination. The sequence
{Γ(ℓ) }M
       ℓ=0 is referred to as the onion decomposition of Γ. Finally, define the remainder sub-
graphs D(ℓ) ≜ Γ(ℓ) \ Γ(ℓ−1) for ℓ = 1, 2, . . . , M .
    Intuitively, this process iteratively selects the densest remaining subgraph, removes it,
                                                                                       |H|
and continues on the remainder. In the case of balanced graphs [Bol81], where maxH⊆Γ |v(H)| =
  |Γ|
|v(Γ)|
       , the process terminates in a single step. Note that the decomposition elements {Γ(ℓ) }ℓ≥0
are, by definition, edge-disjoint; however, they may share vertices. The following result,
proved in Appendix A.2, shows that the decomposition is unique. An alternative proof
appears in [LPRZ25, Thm. 3.6] as well.
Lemma 1 (Uniqueness). Let Γ = Γn be an arbitrary graph, and let {Γ(ℓ) }M                   (ℓ) M
                                                                             ℓ=0 (and {D }ℓ=0 )
denote its onion decomposition in Definition 4. Then, for every ℓ = 0, 1, . . . , M −1, the choice
of Γ(ℓ+1) in step (ii) is unique. Thus, the onion decomposition of any graph is uniquely
determined.
     We note that computing µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) ) for ℓ ∈ [M (Γ)] requires, at least
naively, constructing the entire sequence {Γ(ℓ) }Mℓ=0 . Nonetheless, the following result, proved
in Appendix A.3, provides a non-sequential equivalent characterization. In particular, a key
quantity in our results and analysis is the minimal maximum subgraph density, given by
η(Γ(M ) |Γ(M −1) ), corresponding to the relative density of the final layer in the onion decom-
position of Γ.

Throughout this paper, we sometimes suppress the explicit dependence of various parameters
on the index n. For example, we write the sequence of planted graphs as Γ = (Γn )n , the
sequences of edge probabilities as p = (pn )n and q = (qn )n , and so on.


3      Main Results
In this section, we present our main results. We begin by analyzing the statistical limits
of the recovery problem, setting computational considerations aside, and identify a sharp
threshold at which the optimal recovery error probability undergoes a phase transition from
0 to 1 as n → ∞. We then address recovery under polynomial-time constraints by proposing
a general, computationally efficient procedure and providing statistical guarantees on its per-
formance. Next, we establish several computational lower bounds, showing that polynomial-
time algorithms can incur an inherent gap relative to the optimal (information-theoretic)
solution. Finally, we consider extensions of the vanilla planted model, including recovery
under semi-random (monotone-adversary) perturbations as well as other weaker notions of
recovery.

3.1     Statistical limits
In this subsection, we establish the statistical limits of the problem, beginning with information-
theoretic lower bounds and continuing with matching upper bounds.
Lower bound. We start with the following general lower bound, which holds for an arbi-
trary planted subgraph Γ.

Theorem 1 (Statistical lower bound). Fix a sequence of subgraphs Γ = (Γn )n , and assume
pn , qn = Θ(1). Exact recovery is statistically impossible if

                                                   (1 − ε) · log n
                                    µmin (Γn ) ≤                   ,                        (12)
                                                      dKL (p||q)

for any ε > 0.

      To better understand Theorem 1, we now consider a few examples.


                                                   13
Example 1 (Planted clique). Consider the case where Γ = Kk is a clique with k = |v(Kk )|
vertices. A clique is balanced (i.e., µ(Kk ) = η(Kk ) = k−1          2
                                                                       ), and thus µmin (Kk ) = k−1
                                                                                                 2
                                                                                                    .
Therefore, Theorem 1 tells us that exact recovery is statistically impossible if k ≤ (1 −
    2 log n
ε) dKL (p||q)
              , which is consistent with folklore results (e.g., [Jer92]).

Example 2 (Union of disjoint cliques). Consider the case where Γ is a union of L disjoint
cliques with sizes k1 , . . . , kL . Define kmin ≜ mini∈[L] ki . A straightforward calculation reveals
that µmin (Γ) = kmin2 −1 , and so Theorem 1 implies that exact recovery is statistically impossible
                   2 log n
if kmin ≤ (1 − ε) dKL (p||q)
                             .

Example 3 (A kite). Consider the case where Γ is a kite on k + 1 vertices, namely, a clique
with k = |v(Kk )| vertices, with one of its vertices connected by an edge to an additional vertex.
It is not difficult to see that, in this case, the onion decomposition layers are D(1) = Kk and
D(2) = {·, k + 1}, which is a single edge. Thus, µmin (Kk ) = 21 . Therefore, Theorem 1 tells us
that exact recovery of a kite is statistically impossible. This aligns with intuition: the noisy
random background can alter (or “tweak”) the single edge with positive probability.
     Example 3 illustrates that if Γ contains a dense core together with a very sparse ap-
pendage, then the latter can preclude exact recovery. In such cases, it is reasonable to
consider alternative recovery criteria (such as weak recovery), which allow for a nonzero
fraction of errors. We discuss such criteria in Subsection 3.4. Next, it is instructive to com-
pare and contrast the above results with the detection (hypothesis-testing) variant, given
by

                          H0 : G ∼ G(n, q) vs. H1 : G ∼ GΓn (n, p, q).                           (13)

Here, the task is to decide whether or not a copy of Γ was planted in G. Specifically, [EH25,
Thm. 1] shows that
   • If Γn has sub-logarithmic density, i.e., µ(Γn ) = o(log |v(Γn )|), then detection is statis-
     tically impossible provided that

                                         |e(Γn )| ∨ d2max (Γn ) ≪ n.                             (14)

   • If Γn has super-logarithmic density, i.e., µ(Γn ) = Ω(log |v(Γn )|), then detection is
     statistically impossible provided that

                                            µ(Γn ) ≤ C · log n,                                  (15)

      for some C > 0.
It is further shown in the same paper that these bounds are asymptotically tight, namely,
there exist algorithms that achieve them. Comparing the above with Theorem 1, we see that
the statistical barriers for detection and recovery hinge on different graph-theoretic quanti-
ties, revealing a distinct detection–recovery gap. Specifically, in the sub-logarithmic density
regime, which includes graphs such as paths, stars, trees, and related structures, detection
can be statistically possible, in stark contrast to exact recovery, which is always statisti-
cally impossible in this regime. In fact, this impossibility extends beyond subgraphs with

                                                 14
sub-logarithmic density to (arguably) all subgraphs with sub-logarithmic minimal maximum
subgraph density µmin . Thus, a clear detection–recovery gap emerges in this setting. By
contrast, the super-logarithmic density regime includes sufficiently dense subgraphs, such as
cliques and bipartite graphs, where detection and recovery thresholds may coincide when
the planted structure is “sufficiently nice” (e.g., when Γ is a clique). However, recovery may
still be statistically impossible even when detection is feasible, mirroring the behavior in the
sub-logarithmic regime, as illustrated by the kite example.
      Finally, for the special case where Γ is balanced [Bol81] (e.g., a clique), we can estab-
lish tight information-theoretic lower bounds using a different technique, namely Bayes risk
analysis, which relates exact recovery to a variant of the detection problem. The details are
provided in Appendix C.2 We now move to our statistical upper bounds.
Upper bound. It is relatively straightforward to show that the optimal maximum-likelihood
estimator (MLE) of Γ⋆ is given by (see Appendix B for details)

                                     Γ̂MLE (G) = arg max
                                                     ′
                                                         |e(Γ′ ) ∩ G| .                                     (16)
                                                         Γ ∈SΓ

recursive peeling MLE procedure. Specifically, let {Γ(ℓ) }ℓ≥1 denote the onion decomposition
of Γ in Definition 4, and recall that M(H, Γ) denotes the set of copies of Γ in Kn which
contain H. At step ℓ ∈ [M ], the recovery algorithm produces the MLE estimate for Γ(ℓ) as
follows:
                                                                     
                         (ℓ)                                    (ℓ−1)
                       Γ̂MLE = arg    max        e(D) ∩ G \ Γ̂MLE .                     (17)
                                                 (ℓ−1)
                                         D∈M(Γ̂MLE ,Γ(ℓ) )


That is, the estimator searches for a copy of Γ(ℓ) , restricted to those copies that extend the
                             (ℓ−1)
previously estimated layer Γ̂MLE , within the observed graph G, after removing the previously
                  (ℓ−1)               (0)

                                                              (M )

The following result complements the information-theoretic lower bound in Theorem 1.
Theorem 2 (Optimal algorithm). Fix a sequence of subgraphs Γ = (Γn )n , and assume
pn , qn = Θ(1). Exact recovery of Γ⋆ , via the likelihood peeling algorithm in (18), is possible
if
                                                         (1 + ε) · log n
                                        µmin (Γn ) ≥ C                   ,                                  (19)
                                                           dKL (p||q)
for any ε > 0 and some constant C > 0.
   2
    As noted in [Wei25, pg. 7], even in the special case of a planted clique, the “2 log2 n” information-theoretic
threshold had been established for detection but not for exact recovery, although the latter was expected to
hold. In this work, we confirm this expectation by providing a lecture-style proof that establishes 2 log2 n
as the sharp threshold for exact recovery as well, and in fact we generalize this result to arbitrary planted
subgraphs.


                                                         15
     Thus, Theorem 2 matches the lower bound in Theorem 1 up to a constant factor. For
p = 1, the proof of Theorem 2 yields C = 4, and for any p < 1 we obtain C = 16. We have
not made a serious effort to optimize this constant, but we conjecture that C = 1 for any
p ∈ [0, 1].3
     It is worth emphasizing that the proof of Theorem 2 relies on an important property
of the layers obtained in the onion decomposition of Γ, namely a uniqueness property of
the planted copy on its full vertex set. Specifically, one of the steps in the proof analyzes
|v(D′ ∩ D(ℓ),⋆ )|, the intersection between an isomorphic copy D′ ̸= D(ℓ),⋆ of the actual planted
ℓth layer D(ℓ),⋆ . In principle, it could happen that |v(D′ ∩D(ℓ),⋆ )| = |v(D(ℓ),⋆ )| yet D′ ̸= D(ℓ),⋆ ;
for example, this can occur in the case of a kite. When this happens, exact recovery becomes
impossible. Fortunately, we show that this cannot occur for the layers selected by the onion
procedure when the parameters lie in the achievability regime.

3.2     Computationally efficient algorithm
We now introduce a polynomial-time recovery algorithm. To describe the procedure, we
first establish notation and record several definitions. The Hilbert–Schmidt inner product
(also referred to as the Frobenius inner product) between two matrices A and B of identical
dimensions is
                                                    X
                               ⟨A, B⟩ ≜ Tr(AB⊤ ) =      Ai,j Bi,j .                   (20)
                                                             i,j


This inner product will be used throughout our analysis.
     The formulation in (16) often serves as a starting point for constructing computationally
tractable recovery algorithms, typically via relaxation. In our work, we employ semidefinite
programming (SDP), a matrix-based generalization of linear programming, as one such re-
laxation. A standard form of an SDP is
                                    max      ⟨C, Z⟩
                                   Z∈Rn×n
                                    s.t.     Z⪰0                                                       (21)
                                             ⟨Bi , Z⟩ ≤ βi     ∀i ∈ [m],

for a collection of matrices C, {Bi }m                    m
                                     i=1 , constants {βi }i=1 , and m ∈ N. Here, Z ⪰ 0 means
that Z is symmetric and positive semidefinite. SDPs are convex optimization problems and
therefore admit efficient solution techniques, including interior-point methods and first-order
algorithms; see, for instance, [NN94, BV04]. A typical use case is to approximate solutions
to optimization problems with nonconvex constraints, such as integer programs, by relaxing
them into a semidefinite form.
     Recall that the nuclear norm ∥B∥⋆ of a matrix B is defined as the ℓ1 -norm of its singular
value vector. When B is symmetric, this equals the sum of the absolute values of its eigenval-
ues. Slightly abusing notation, we denote by ∥G∥⋆ the nuclear norm of the adjacency matrix
associated with a graph G, interpreted as the adjacency matrix of the subgraph induced on
   3
     As remarked in the proof, improving the constant would require splitting the various sums involved into
different asymptotic regimes and analyzing each case separately.


                                                      16
|v(G)| vertices. Importantly, if |v(G)| ≤ n, then padding the adjacency matrix with zeros to
embed G into an n-vertex graph does not change its nuclear norm.
      To facilitate the discussion, we associate the planted subgraph Γ⋆ with a clustered
adjacency matrix X⋆ ∈ {0, 1}n×n , defined such that X⋆ii = 0 for all i ∈ [n], X⋆ij = 1 if
(i, j) ∈ e(Γ), and X⋆ij = 0 otherwise. Thus, X⋆ can be interpreted as the adjacency matrix of
Γ⋆ embedded in a graph on n vertices, where the additional n − |v(Γ⋆ )| vertices are isolated.
Note that there is a one-to-one correspondence between each Γ⋆ and its matrix representation
X⋆ .
      Our main result depends on spectral and structural properties of real-valued symmet-
ric matrices. Let Q be a generic real-valued symmetric matrix. Denote its singular value
decomposition (SVD) by Q = UQ ΣUTQ , where Σ ≜ Diag(σ1 , . . . , σr ) ∈ Rr×r contains the (non-
negative) singular values {σi }ri=1 . The columns of UQ = [u1 , . . . , ur ] ∈ Rn×r are orthonormal
and are referred to as the singular vectors. The quantity r ≜ rank(Q) denotes the rank of Q.
      In our setting, Q will exhibit a block structure; for example,
                                                     
                                                QS 0
                                          Q=            ,                                       (22)
                                                0 0

where QS denotes the restriction of Q to S ⊆ [n]. Accordingly, UQ is supported on S, in the
sense that [UQ ]i,: = 0 for all i ∈
                                  / S. Finally, we define the coherence parameter of UQ , which
measures how “spread out” (or “localized”) its rows are:
                                     n                            n
                    coh(UQ ) ≜           · max ∥[UQ ]i,: ∥22 =         · ∥[UQ ]i,: ∥22,∞ .        (23)

We prove in Appendix A.4 that |S|   ≤ coh(UQ ) ≤ rank(Q) .4 With a slight abuse of notation,
we also write coh(Q) = coh(UQ ). In our presentation and analysis, it is convenient to work
with the following transformation of A:

    To present the proposed algorithm and its performance guarantees, we introduce a few
additional pieces of notation. Fix a hyper-parameter α ∈ R+ . For any symmetric matrix
X ∈ Rn×n with Diag(X) = 0, and any vector s ∈ Rn , define

                                       Ext(X, s; α) ≜ X + α · Diag(s).                            (25)

Let s⋆ = supp(v(Γ⋆ )) denote the support of the vertex set of the planted subgraph. At
(X, s) = (X⋆ , s⋆ ), the matrix Ext(X⋆ , s⋆ ; α) is therefore a diagonally shifted version of the
adjacency matrix of Γ⋆ . We denote the nuclear norm of the diagonally shifted version of an
arbitrary planted subgraph Γ ∈ SΓ by

                               ∥Ext(X⋆ , s⋆ ; α)∥⋆ = ∥Γ + α · Diag(v(Γ))∥⋆ .                      (26)
   4
       Note that the lower bound is achieved when Γ⋆ is a clique; in this case X⋆ has rank one with U =
√ 1 ⋆ 1v(Γ⋆ ) , and hence coh(U) = |v(Γn⋆ )| .
 |v(Γ )|


                                                      17
This quantity is an a priori known constant, taking the same value for all Γ ∈ SΓ , and is
independent of the particular latent planted subgraph.
    We now consider the following convex optimization problem:
                X̂(α)
                  con =     arg max       ⟨W, X⟩
                       X∈Rn×n ,s∈[0,1]n

                          s.t.            s⊤ 1 = |v(Γ)|, Xij ≤ min(si , sj ), ∀i ̸= j
                                          ∥Ext(X, s; α)∥⋆ ≤ ∥Γ + α · Diag(v(Γ))∥⋆          (27)

                                          0 ≤ X ≤ J, X = X⊤ , Xii = 0, ∀i ∈ [n]
                                          ⟨J, X⟩ = 2|e(Γ)|,
where the inequality 0 ≤ X ≤ J is to be interpreted entrywise. In several special cases, such
as when Γ is a clique or a bipartite graph, the program in (27) can be viewed as a direct
relaxation of the maximum-likelihood estimator.
      Before proceeding, we briefly interpret the variables and constraints in (27). The vector
s ∈ [0, 1]n serves as a relaxed selector for the vertex set of the planted subgraph: each
coordinate si indicates whether vertex i belongs to v(Γ⋆ ). At the planted solution, s⋆i =
1 {i ∈ v(Γ⋆ )}. Similarly, X ∈ Rn×n is a convex relaxation of the planted adjacency matrix
X⋆ . One might ask why the auxiliary variable s is needed, rather than encoding the vertex set
directly via self-loops in X. The issue is that such an encoding can significantly weaken the
discriminative power of the nuclear norm. For instance, certain subgraphs, such as complete
bipartite graphs, become full-rank under this representation, rendering the nuclear-norm
prior ineffective. Introducing
                        P         s allows us to control the number of selected vertices through
the linear constraint      i∈[n] i = |v(Γ)|, while applying the low-rank prior to the shifted
                                s
matrix Ext(X, s; α).
      In addition, we impose the edge–vertex coupling constraints Xij ≤ min(si , sj ) for all
i ̸= j, ensuring that an edge can exist only if both endpoints are selected. These are convex
(McCormick envelope-type) relaxations of the nonconvex constraint Xij ≤ si sj , enforcing
that X is supported on the selected vertex set. The remaining constraints ensure that X
encodes a relaxed simple-graph adjacency matrix: it is symmetric, has zero diagonal, and
has fixed off-diagonal mass equal to 2|e(Γ)|, corresponding to the number of edges in the
planted subgraph. Finally, we constrain the nuclear norm of the shifted matrix Ext(X, s; α)
to be no larger than that of the ground-truth planted subgraph. This promotes low-rank
structure in the solution. By choosing α appropriately, we can avoid rank inflation caused
by forced self-loops. For example, setting α = 1 for cliques ensures that Γ + α · Diag(v(Γ))
has rank one, while setting α = 0 for complete bipartite graphs ensures it has rank two.
      The program (27) is convex. The objective ⟨W, X⟩ is linear in X, and all constraints
on X and s are linear, except for the nuclear-norm constraint. The latter defines a convex
set because ∥ · ∥⋆ is a convex norm and Ext(·, ·; α) is affine. Moreover, the nuclear-norm
constraint admits a standard semidefinite representation, allowing (27) to be formulated as
a semidefinite program (SDP). In particular, the following classical result applies.
Lemma 3 ([Faz02, Lemma 2]). Fix t ≥ 0. Let X ∈ Rm×n . Then, ∥X∥⋆ ≤ t if and only if
there exist P1 ∈ Rm×m and P2 ∈ Rn×n such that,
                              
                         P1 X
                                 ⪰ 0 and Tr(P1 ) + Tr(P2 ) ≤ 2t.              (28)
                         X⊤ P2

                                                   18

admits a polynomial-time solution via standard convex optimization methods. We now state
the following result.

3.3     Computational lower bounds
In this subsection, we derive computational lower bounds for recovery. We begin by intro-
ducing the low-degree polynomial (LDP) framework for recovery, setting up the notation
used throughout, and recording several basic identities that will be invoked later. Our pre-
sentation adapts the general LDP methodology to the planted-graph model from Section 2,
and follows [SW22] (see also [BKW22]).

3.3.1   Background and preliminaries
Problem setup. Fix n, 0 < qn < pn ≤ 1, and a planted structure Γn = (v(Γn ), e(Γn ))
with |v(Γn )| ≤ n and no isolated vertices. A planted copy Γ⋆n ∈ SΓn is selected uniformly at
random and then observed through the binary edge–channel
                                                    (
                                                       pn , e ∈ e(Γ⋆n )
                       Ye | Γ⋆n ∼ Bern(Xe ),   Xe =                                      (42)
                                                              / e(Γ⋆n ),

independently over e ∈ [n]                                   n
                                                             
                         2
                             . We write N ≜                  2
                                                                  and view Y ∈ {0, 1}N as the input to any
                     N
estimator Γ̂ : {0, 1} → SΓn .




                                                         21
LDP and performance metrics. We observe a random vector Y ∈ Y N and seek to
estimate an anchor scalar parameter x ∈ R, defined as a function of the latent signal
generating Y (e.g., one coordinate of the signal). For a degree budget D ∈ N, let
              R[Y]≤D ≜ f : Y N → R polynomial of total degree at most D ,
                          
                                                                                  (43)
namely, the space of polynomials of degree at most D. The degree-D minimum mean-squared
error is defined as
                           MMSE≤D ≜ inf E (f (Y) − x)2 ,
                                                             
                                                                                   (44)
                                           f ∈R[Y]≤D

where the expectation is taken with respect to the joint law of (x, Y). Furthermore, define
the associated degree-D maximum correlation as
                                                      E [f (Y) · x]
                               Corr≤D ≜       sup     p             .                     (45)
                                            f ∈R[Y]≤D   E [f (Y)2 ]
A basic identity links these two quantities:
                                MMSE≤D = E[x2 ] − Corr2≤D .                               (46)
This identity allows us to lower bound MMSE≤D (and hence derive computational lower
bounds) by upper bounding Corr≤D . Polynomials of degree D = polylog(n) capture a
broad class of efficient estimators, including many spectral and AMP-type procedures via
polynomial approximation, and the LDP framework has proved predictive for computa-
tional thresholds across a range of high-dimensional problems [SW22]. In particular, if the
right-hand side of (45) is o(1) for some D = polylog(n), then Corr≤D = o(1) and hence
MMSE≤D ≥ E[x2 ] − o(1), ruling out any degree-D polynomial from achieving nontrivial
recovery of x. This yields concrete, model-specific computational lower bounds that often
match (up to polylog factors) the best known algorithmic thresholds in classical inference
problems.
     To study recovery via low-degree polynomials, we focus on a one-bit anchor that is
symmetric across the vertices of the planted copy. Fix an ambient anchor vertex v ⋆ = {1}
and define
                                 x ≜ 1{v ⋆ ∈ v(Γ⋆n )} ∈ {0, 1}.                           (47)
Because Γ⋆n is drawn uniformly among all of its isomorphic copies, the joint law of (x, Y) does
not depend on which ambient vertex is chosen, and the anchor is without loss of generality.
Binary observation model. The planted graph problem in this paper fits the general
binary model considered in [SW22, Sec. 2.3], where coordinates of Y are conditionally
independent Bernoulli variables with means X = (Xi )i∈[N ] taking values in {τ0 , τ1 } with
0 < τ0 < τ1 < 1. In this setting, [SW22, Thm. 2.7] derived an upper bound on Corr≤D
expressed in terms of joint cumulants:
                                            X                κ2α

                                               X

3.3.2    Main result
The following result shows that O(log n)-degree polynomials fail at recovery whenever the
                                 |e(Γn )|  √

assume pn , qn = Θ(1). If

number of iterations L ∈ N. For simplicity of notation, let Zij ≜ Yij − q, for any i, j ∈ [n].

Example 9 (Planted clique). Consider the case where Γn = Kk . Since |v(Γn )|

the state-of-the-art algorithmic threshold (see Example 4) and consistent with the folklore

3.4     Extensions
In this subsection, we present several direct extensions of the results above. We begin by
showing that both the optimal and the efficient algorithms we introduce are robust to a
simple monotone adversary, which is allowed to remove arbitrary edges outside the planted
subgraph and add edges within it. We then develop guarantees for weaker notions of recovery.

3.4.1   Semi-random model
We now consider a framework for the recovery problem in a semi-random graph setting
known as the monotone adversary or “Sandwich Model” introduced in [FK00]. As before,
let Γ = (Γn )n be a sequence of graphs with |v(Γn )| ≤ n. To exclude trivial settings, we
assume that Γn contains no isolated vertices. The recovery task is to exactly identify a
hidden subgraph embedded in a noisy background graph under semi-random perturbations.
     We assume the data is generated according to the following semi-random process. First,
we pick a subgraph Γ⋆ ∈ SΓ . A random graph G is then formed by retaining each edge of

                                                25
Γ⋆ independently with probability p, and adding each edge outside Γ⋆ independently with
probability q. The resulting distribution over graphs is denoted GΓn (n, p, q). An adversary
may then modify G by removing edges that do not belong to the planted structure Γ⋆ , and
by adding edges within Γ⋆ . The final observed graph is denoted by GAdv . We model this
perturbation via a (possibly randomized) function Adv acting on both G and Γ⋆ , such that
                                 n
GAdv = Adv(G, Γ⋆ ) ∈ {0, 1}( 2 ) . The adversary satisfies
                                                                                             
                    \                                          \
                                AGAdv (i, j) ≤ AG (i, j),             AGAdv (i, j) ≥ AG (i, j) = 1. (65)
                                                                                             
          P
                                                                   ⋆
             (i,j)∈([n]
                     2 )
                        \e(Γ⋆ )                           (i,j)∈e(Γ )


We denote by A the collection of all such valid (possibly randomized) functions Adv satisfying
the condition above. When randomized, these functions define conditional distributions over
graphs. Thus, the observed graph is drawn from a distribution in the family Adv(GΓn (n, p, q)),
induced by applying some Adv ∈ A to a sample from the planted model. Our goal is to design
a recovery algorithm that identifies the underlying subgraph Γ⋆ exactly.
                                                     n
     A recovery algorithm is a function Γ̂n : {0, 1}( 2 ) → SΓ that maps an observed graph to
a candidate subgraph. The (worst-case) error of such an algorithm is given by
                             Eadv (Γ̂) ≜ sup        sup P[Γ̂(GAdv1 ) ̸= Γ⋆ ].                       (66)
                                         Γ⋆ ∈SΓ Adv1 ∈A1

We define the worst-case optimal error as
                                     E⋆adv ≜         inf           Eadv (ϕ).                        (67)
                                                       (2)
                                               Γ̂:{0,1}n     →SΓ

As before, we say that exact recovery is possible if lim supn→∞ E⋆adv = 0, and impossible
otherwise.
     It is straightforward to show that the optimal MLE in the vanilla setting (i.e., without
an adversary) is robust to the adversarial model described above. This is summarized in the
following result.
Theorem 6 (MLE is robust). Let Γ̂MLE denote the MLE for the recovery problem in Sec-
tion 2. Then, whenever Γ̂MLE = Γ⋆ is the unique solution to the recovery problem under
GΓn (n, p, q) (i.e., in the absence of an adversary), it remains the unique solution under the
adversarial mode Adv(GΓn (n, p, q)).
     In other words, Eadv (Γ̂MLE ) → 0 whenever E(Γ̂MLE ) → 0. In light of the fact that
E⋆ ≤ E⋆adv , we see that, at least statistically, the monotone adversary does not alter the
performance.
Proof of Theorem 6. Recall that the MLE is given by
                                  Γ̂MLE (G) = arg max
                                                  ′
                                                      |e(Γ′ ) ∩ G| .                                (68)
                                                       Γ ∈SΓ

Denote the corresponding G after modification as Gadv . Then, for all feasible Γ ̸= Γ⋆ , we
have the following chain of inequalities:
                |e(Γ) ∩ Gadv | = |e(Γ ∩ (Γ⋆ )c ) ∩ Gadv | + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv |                    (69)

                                                      26
                            ≤ |e(Γ ∩ (Γ⋆ )c ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv |                  (70)
                            = |e(Γ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv | − |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ G|         (71)
                            < |e(Γ⋆ ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv | − |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ G|       (72)
                            = |e(Γc ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ G| + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv |                     (73)
                            ≤ |e(Γc ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv | + |e(Γ ∩ Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv |                 (74)
                            = |e(Γ⋆ ) ∩ Gadv | ,                                           (75)
where the second inequality follows from the fact that outside the planted subgraph (i.e., in
Γ ∩ (Γ⋆ )c ), the monotone adversary can only remove edges. Therefore, replacing Gadv with G
can only increase the intersection. The second strict inequality holds due to the assumption
that Γ⋆ is the unique global maximizer of the original problem in the absence of an adversary.
The third inequality follows from the fact that within the planted subgraph (i.e., in Γc ∩ Γ⋆ ),
the adversary can only add edges, so replacing G with Gadv can only increase the intersection.
Hence, Γ⋆ stays optimal even when the graph is modified by the adversary.
    Next, we show that the computationally efficient algorithm proposed in (27) is also
robust to a monotone adversary.
                                                    (α)
Theorem 7 (Convex program is robust). Let X̂con denote the convex program in (27). Then,
whenever Γ⋆ is the unique solution of (27) to the recovery problem under GΓn (n, p, q) (i.e.,
in the absence of an adversary), it remains the unique solution under the adversarial mode
Adv(GΓn (n, p, q)).
Proof of Theorem 7. The proof follows the same ideas as in the proof of Theorem 6. Denote
the corresponding W after modification as Wadv . Also, recall that X⋆ denotes the adjacency
matrix of the planted subgraph Γ⋆ . Let X⋆comp denote the adjacency matrix of (Γ⋆ )c , and for
any feasible X let Xcomp ≜ J − X. Finally, recall that ⊙ denotes the element-wise Hadamard
product. Then, for all feasible X ̸= X⋆ , we have the following chain of inequalities:
                       ⟨Wadv , X⟩ = Wadv , X ⊙ X⋆comp + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                   (76)
                                 ≤   W, X ⊙ X⋆comp                       ⋆
                                                          + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X ⟩                (77)
                                 = ⟨W, X⟩ + ⟨Wadv − W, X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                            (78)
                                 < ⟨W, X⋆ ⟩ + ⟨Wadv − W, X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                          (79)
                                 = ⟨W, X⋆ ⊙ Xcomp ⟩ + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                     (80)
                                 ≤ ⟨Wadv , X⋆ ⊙ Xcomp ⟩ + ⟨Wadv , X ⊙ X⋆ ⟩                 (81)
                                 = ⟨Wadv , X⋆ ⟩ ,                                          (82)
where the second inequality arises because outside the planted subgraph (that is, over the
region X ⊙ X⋆comp ), the monotone adversary is limited to deleting edges. As a result, substi-
tuting Wadv with W can only lead to a larger objective value over this region. The second
strict inequality follows from the premise that X⋆ is the unique global maximizer of the orig-
inal problem when no adversary is present. The third inequality holds because within the
planted subgraph (specifically, over X⋆ ⊙Xcomp ), the adversary is only allowed to insert edges.
Consequently, replacing W with Wadv can only increase the objective contribution over this
region, implying that X⋆ remains the optimal solution even under adversarial modifications
to the graph.

                                               27
3.4.2     Individual layers recovery
The strict notion of exact recovery precludes recovering subgraphs with very sparse layers
(e.g., as in Example 3). One way to bypass this inherent limitation is to consider weaker
notions of recovery. For instance, one might be interested in exactly recovering only the
subgraph H ⊆ Γ that achieves the maximum subgraph density of Γ. More generally, for any
κ ∈ [M (Γ)]—where
                SM M (Γ)      denotes the number of components in the onion decomposition
                         (ℓ)
of Γ, i.e., Γ = ℓ=1 D S      (see Definition 4)—we could aim to exactly recover the first κ
layers of Γ, namely Γ = κℓ=1 D(ℓ) . Accordingly, we define the worst-case error probability

that κ-exact recovery is impossible if lim inf n→∞ En > 0. Finally, we define the truncated
likelihood peeling algorithm as
                                               (κ)        (κ)

following result.

Theorem 8 (κ-exact recovery). Fix a sequence of subgraphs Γ = (Γn )n , κ ∈ [M (Γ)], and
assume pn , qn = Θ(1). Consider the onion decomposition of Γ in Definition 4.

  1. κ-exact recovery is statistically impossible if

                                                          (1 − ε) · log n
                                     µ(Γ|Γ(κ−1) ) ≤                       ,                 (85)

        for any ε > 0.

  2. κ-exact recovery of Γ⋆ , via the truncated likelihood peeling algorithm in (84), is possible
     if

                                                          (1 + ε) · log n
                                    µ(Γ|Γ(κ−1) ) ≥ C                      ,                 (86)
                                                            dKL (p||q)

        for any ε > 0 and some constant C > 0.

     Going back to Example 3, if we take κ = 1, this corresponds to recovering the clique
while ignoring the single edge attached to it (which precluded strong recovery). Since D(1) =
Kk , Theorem 8 tells us that 1-exact recovery is possible (or impossible) when k ≷ C log n for
some C > 0, just as in the planted clique recovery problem. Finally, the proof of Theorem 8
follows verbatim from the arguments used in the proofs of Theorems 1 and 2, with the
recovery restricted to Γ(κ) . The details are therefore omitted.

                                                     28
3.4.3     Almost-exact recovery
Next, we turn to the notion of almost-exact recovery, defined as follows (see, e.g., [HWX17,
WX20]).

Definition 5 (Almost-exact recovery). An estimator Γ̂ almost-exactly recovers Γ⋆ if, as
n → ∞, dH (Γ̂, Γ⋆ )/|e(Γ)| → 0 in probability, where dH denotes the Hamming distance between
the adjacency matrices of Γ̂ and Γ⋆ .

     In general, lower bounds for exact recovery do not directly imply lower bounds for
almost-exact recovery, but any estimator that achieves exact recovery also achieves almost-
exact recovery. To state our main results, we first introduce some notation. Consider the
onion decomposition of Γn in Definition 4, and define for ℓ ∈ 0 ∪ [M (Γ)] and n ∈ N the ℓth
leftover-edge fraction as
                                                                  (ℓ)
                                            (n)       |e(Γn \ Γn )|
                                       Resℓ       ≜                 .                         (87)
                                                         |e(Γn )|

Fix any null sequence εn ↓ 0 and define
                                                                          (n)
                          ℓLB (n) ≜ max{ℓ ∈ 0 ∪ [M (Γ)] : Resℓ                  > εn },       (88)
                                                                         (n)
                         ℓUB (n) ≜ min{ℓ ∈ 0 ∪ [M (Γ)] : Resℓ                   ≤ εn }.       (89)

Heuristically, ℓLB is the last index for which the leftover-edge fraction is Ω(1), whereas ℓUB
                                                                          (n)
is the first index for which the leftover-edge fraction is o(1). Since Resℓ is nonincreasing, it
follows that ℓUB (n) = ℓLB (n) + 1. We nevertheless introduce both notations for clarity.
Lower bounds. We begin with our impossibility results. For almost-exact recovery, an
interesting dichotomy emerges. Roughly speaking, if the last non-negligible leftover compo-
nent has sub-logarithmic maximum subgraph relative density, then almost-exact recovery is
impossible. On the other hand, when this relative density is super-logarithmic, almost-exact
recovery remains impossible whenever it is at most logarithmic in n. This dichotomy is
summarized in the following result, proved in Appendix D.

Theorem 9. Fix a sequence of subgraphs Γ = (Γn )n , assume pn , qn = Θ(1), and consider
the onion decomposition of Γ in Definition 4.

  1. If |v(Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) )| = o(n) and

                                                            log |v(Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) )|
                                                                                     
                                       (ℓLB )
                               µ(Γ|Γ            )=o                                       ,   (90)
                                                          log log |v(Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) )|

        then almost-exact recovery is impossible.

  2. If µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) ≥ αn · log |v(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) )|, for some αn = Ω(1), then there exists a constant
     C > 0 such that almost-exact recovery is impossible if

                                           µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) ≤ C · log n.                         (91)

                                                       29
     A few remarks are in order. First, the proofs of (90) and (91) rely on different techniques,
each tailored to its own regime of relative density and typically suboptimal in the other. In
both cases, the first step is to reduce almost-exact recovery for Γ to recovering the last
non-negligible Ω(1) leftover-edge fraction of Γ, namely, Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) .
     To prove (90), one of the main ingredients is a generalization of the subgraph expectation
threshold [KK07], and more specifically, of the modified subgraph expectation threshold
studied in [MNWSZ22], which analyzes the threshold for the appearance of any isomorphic
copy of Γ in G ∼ G(n, q). For our purposes, however, not all copies are admissible. Indeed,
recall that the recovery problem here is supplied with Γ(ℓLB ) and is tasked with finding Γ.
Thus, the admissible copies are precisely those extending Γ(ℓLB ) , i.e., the copies contained in
M(Γ(ℓLB ) , Γ). To handle this, we derive a generalization of the modified subgraph expectation
threshold that accounts for the appearance of such constrained copies.
     To prove (91), we establish a connection between almost-exact recovery and a hypothesis-
testing variant of the problem (see (13)), and then leverage the known impossibility result
(15) for the latter. Most notably, as discussed right after (14)–(15), in the sub-logarithmic
regime there exists a region where detection is statistically possible while, as we show above,
almost-exact recovery is always impossible. This highlights why hypothesis-testing–based
bounds are insufficient in this regime.
     Let us now explain the scope of Theorem 9. Comparing Theorems 1 and 9, we see that
if Γ is balanced and has super-logarithmic maximum density, i.e., µ(Γn ) = Ω(log |v(Γn )|),
then the two bounds coincide (up to constant factors). In this case, almost-exact and exact
recovery are statistically equivalent. In general, however, the bounds need not coincide.
As noted after Theorem 1 (see, e.g., Example 3), the strict exact-recovery criterion rules
out recovering graphs with a dense core and a very sparse appendage, whereas almost-exact
recovery may remain possible since one may effectively “discard” the problematic appendage,
provided its contribution is o(|e(Γn )|). We illustrate this phenomenon with two examples.
Example 12 (A kite). Recall Example 3, where Γ is a kite on k + 1 vertices—namely, a
clique with k = |v(Kk )| vertices, with one of its vertices connected by an edge to an additional
vertex. In this case, ℓLB = 0, so Γ(ℓLB ) = ∅ and the core is a clique on k vertices. Thus,
Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) = Γ and µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) = µ(Γ) = µ(Kk ) = k−1     2
                                                              . Hence, almost-exact recovery is
impossible precisely when recovery of a k-clique is impossible, namely for k ≤ C log n for
some constant C > 0, in agreement with folklore results. Therefore, while the dangling edge
precludes exact recovery, it has no effect on almost-exact recovery.
Example 13 (A kite graph with a long tail). Consider the case where Γ is a clique Kk on
k = |v(Kk )| vertices, with one of its vertices attached to a path of length k 2 (i.e., k 2 − 1 edges
and k 2 vertices). In this case, Γ(1) = Kk and Γ(2) = Pk2 (a path on k 2 vertices). Accordingly,
                                                                                 |e(Pk2 )|      2 −1
we have ℓLB = 1, so Γ(ℓLB ) = Kk . Thus, Γ \ Γ(ℓLB ) = Pk2 and µ(Γ|Γ(ℓLB ) ) = k+k2 −k−1    = kk2 −1 =
1. Hence, we are in the sub-logarithmic regime of (90), which implies that almost-exact
recovery is impossible, in agreement with the exact-recovery criterion. We see that a sparse
appendage may still deteriorate almost-exact recovery when its “size” is comparable to that of
the dense core. Notably, if the path above had length o(k 2 ), then almost-exact recovery would
be impossible only when recovery of the clique itself is impossible (whereas exact recovery
remains impossible).
     We now turn to achievability results and show that the lower bounds above are tight.

                                                 30
Upper bounds. Recall the truncated likelihood peeling algorithm in (84). For almost-
exact recovery, we propose
                                                       (ℓ   )
                                         Γ̂almost    UB
                                           pMLE ≜ Γ̂MLE ,                                 (92)
         (ℓ)
where {Γ̂MLE }ℓ≥1 is defined in (17), and ℓUB is defined in (89). Indeed, (92) aims to recover
all but the last layers whose leftover-edge fraction is o(1), since these contribute negligibly
to the Hamming error. We have the following result.
Theorem 10. Almost-exact recovery of Γ⋆ , via the truncated likelihood peeling algorithm in
(92), is possible if
                                                      (1 + ε) · log n
                                µ(Γ|Γ(ℓUB −1) ) ≥ C                   ,                   (93)
                                                        dKL (p||q)
for any ε > 0 and some constant C > 0.
     Since ℓUB = ℓLB + 1, we see that Theorem 10 matches the lower bound in (91) up to a
constant factor. Finally, the proof of Theorem 10 follows verbatim from the arguments used
in the proof of Theorem 2, with the recovery restricted to Γ(ℓUB ) . The details are therefore
omitted.


4    Statistical Lower Bound
In this subsection, we prove Theorem 1. As it turns out, this result can be established using
two different approaches. The first relies on information-theoretic tools, while the second
is based on a second-moment Bayes risk analysis. Here, we present the first approach and
relegate the second to Appendix C.
     We adopt an information-theoretic strategy based on Fano’s inequality combined with
a genie-aided argument. Specifically, we lower bound the optimal recovery error probability
by the error probability of an estimator that is granted partial access to the latent planted
subgraph Γ⋆ . Let {Γ(ℓ),⋆ }M                                                  ⋆

additional information, recovering Γ⋆ reduces to recovering only the final layer
