     Before we plug in the above and sum over the index i, there is a subtle point that
must be taken into account—namely, the uniqueness property of the planted copy on its full
vertex set. Specifically, in theory, it could be the case that |v(Wj ∩ Γ(ℓ),⋆ ) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ )| =
|v(Γ(ℓ),⋆ ) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ )|, yet Wj ̸= Γ(ℓ),⋆ . For example, consider the case where Γ(ℓ),⋆ is a clique
plus a single edge attached to one of its vertices. This scenario implies that the range of
i should be 0 ≤ i ≤ |v(Γ(ℓ),⋆ ) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ )|. However, this possibility is precluded by the
property that, at each step of the onion decomposition (see Definition 4), the selected layer
maximizes the relative subgraph density. We now prove that this is indeed the case. The
following result is general and holds for any step in the onion decomposition of Γ as defined
in Definition 4. Accordingly, consider the ℓth step in the onion decomposition, and recall
that D(ℓ),⋆ ≜ Γ(ℓ),⋆ \ Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ .
Lemma 4 (Uniqueness at full overlap). Consider the onion decomposition of Γ⋆ in Def-
inition 4. Let W ′ ∈ M(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ , Γ(ℓ) ) and D′ = W ′ \ Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ . Assume that D′ satisfies
v(D′ ) = v(D(ℓ),⋆ ). Then e(D′ ) = e(D(ℓ),⋆ ); hence D′ = D(ℓ),⋆ . In particular, there exists no
copy distinct from the planted one whose vertex overlap with D(ℓ),⋆ equals |v(D(ℓ),⋆ )|.
Proof of Lemma 4. By definition of M(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ , Γ(ℓ) ) there is an isomorphism ϕ1 : v(D(ℓ) ) →
v(D(ℓ),⋆ ) with ϕ1 (e(D(ℓ) )) = e(D(ℓ),⋆ ). Furthermore, because we assume that there exist
W ′ ∈ M(Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ , Γ(ℓ) ) such that D′ = W ′ \ Γ(ℓ−1),⋆ with v(D′ ) = v(D(ℓ),⋆ ), there is an
isomorphism ϕ2 : v(D(ℓ) ) → v(D(ℓ),⋆ ) with ϕ2 (e(D(ℓ) )) = e(D′ ). Compose them to obtain an
automorphism ψ = ϕ−1                    (ℓ)           (ℓ)
                            1 ◦ ϕ2 : v(D ) → v(D ). Now, let us show that ψ fixes v(Γ

Remark 2 (Why the argument fails for the whole graph Γ). The conclusion of Lemma 4
relies on the edge–maximality property (3) of each layer Γ(ℓ) . The full graph Γ need not be
edge–maximal on its vertex set. If Γ contains a dense core and a sparse appendage (e.g.,
a clique Kk plus one extra edge), one can keep the entire vertex set fixed and relocate only
the sparse part, producing Θ(n2 ) distinct copies Γ′ ̸= Γ⋆ with full vertex overlap i = |v(Γ⋆ )|.
These copies must be included in the global first–moment sum, and their aggregate contribu-
tion is already unbounded, so the counting/Markov argument used for a peeling layer cannot
be transferred verbatim to the global MLE.

Proof of Theorem 3. We will show that, with high probability, the objective function of any
feasible X ̸= X⋆ is inferior, i.e.,

                                      ⟨X, W⟩ < ⟨X⋆ , W⟩ .                              (160)

To that end, we start by establishing some notations. Denote the singular value decompo-
sition (SVD) of the underlying diagonally shifted adjacency matrix S⋆ ≜ Ext(X⋆ , s⋆ ; α) =
X⋆ + αDiag(s⋆ ) associated with the planted subgraph Γ⋆ by

                                         S⋆ = UΣU⊤ ,                                   (161)

and define the projections projections onto the tangent space T of the manifold of matrices
as PT (M ) ≜ UUT M + M UUT − UUT M UUT and PT ⊥ (M ) ≜ M − PT (M ). We note that
EW = cX⋆ with c ≜ pq − 1 > 0. With these notations, we can write,

   ⟨X⋆ − X, W⟩ = ⟨X⋆ − X, cX⋆ ⟩ + ⟨X⋆ − X, PT ⊥ (W − E[W])⟩ + ⟨X⋆ − X, PT (W − E[W])⟩,

6     Computational Bounds
6.1       Lower bound
We follow the same notations and definitions established in Section 3. Recall that our goal
is to upper bound
                                                  X               κ2α
                           Corr2≤D ≤                                         ,              (212)
                                                           (qn (1 − pn ))|α|
                                         α∈{0,1}N :|α|≤D

where κα = E[x · Xα ] − 0≤β⪇α κβ E[Xα−β ]. Equivalently, κα is the joint cumulant of one
                         P
copy of x and the set of coordinates {Xe : αe = 1}. Therefore, we see that the task of upper
bounding the correlation reduces to the problem of upper bounding the joint cumulants. To
that end, we introduce a few important notations.

                                                      45
Definition 6 (Rooted pattern). A rooted pattern is a finite simple graph H = (v(H), e(H))
together with a distinguished vertex r⋆ ∈ v(H), called the root. We say that H is connected
if the underlying graph is connected.
    We recall the definitions of injective homomorphism and embedding
Definition 7 (Injective homomorphism and embedding). Let G = (v(G), e(G)) be a finite
graph. An injective homomorphism (or embedding) ψ : H ,→ G is an injective map ψ :
v(H) → v(G) such that
                          {u, v} ∈ e(H) =⇒ {ψ(u), ψ(v)} ∈ e(G).                          (213)
Thus, an embedding preserves adjacency and does not identify distinct vertices of H.
Definition 8 (Root-preserving embedding). Let H = (v(H), e(H), r⋆ ) be a rooted pattern and
let v ∈ v(G) be a vertex of a graph v(G). A root-preserving embedding of H into G with root
at v is an injective homomorphism ψ : H ,→ G such that ψ(r⋆ ) = v.
Definition 9 (Rooted embedding counts). Fix integers t ≥ 2 and d ≥ 1. For a template
graph Γn and a vertex v ∈ v(Γn ), we define
        Embt,d (H → Γn , v) ≜ |{ψ : H ,→ Γn : H is a connected r⋆ -rooted pattern with
                                              |v(H)| = t, |e(H)| = d, ψ(r⋆ ) = v}| .     (214)
That is, Embt,d (H → Γn ; v) is the number of root-preserving embeddings of all connected
rooted patterns with t vertices and d edges, where the root of the pattern is mapped to the
specified vertex v of Γn . We also define the total count
                                              X
                          Embt,d (H → Γn ) ≜        Embt,d (H → Γn ; v),              (215)
                                                   v∈v(Γn )

which sums over all possible root locations in Γn . Finally, we define
                                            X
                    Embt,d (Γn ; v) ≜                 Embt,d (H → Γn ; v),               (216)
                                        H: |v(H)|=t, |e(H)|=d
                                        H is connected root r⋆
                                         X
                       Embt,d (Γn ) ≜              Embt,d (Γn ; v).                      (217)
                                        v∈v(Γn )

     The role of the root in the above definition is to enforce alignment with a fixed anchor
vertex in the ambient graph (say vertex 1 ∈ [n]). The distinction between Embt,d (H → Γn , v)
and Embt,d (H → Γn ) is that the former counts embeddings anchored at a specific template
vertex, while the latter sums over all template roots. Next, let H be a rooted pattern with
t vertices and d edges. Let ϕ : v(Γn ) ,→ [n] be a uniformly random injection, and set
Γ⋆n = ϕ(Γn ). Consider the event
               E(H) ≜ {∃ v ∈ v(Γn ) and ψ : H ,→ Γn injective with ψ(r⋆ ) = v,
                                            and (ϕ ◦ ψ)(r⋆ ) = v ⋆ } .                   (218)
Intuitively, E(H) is the event that the rooted pattern H appears inside the planted copy Γ⋆n
with the root landing at the ambient anchor v ⋆ . The following lemma is a key ingredient in
the proof of our upper bound on Corr≤D .

                                                     46
Lemma 7. For any connected rooted pattern H as above,

                                             1       Embt,d (H → Γn )
                             P [E(H)] =            ·                  ,                    (219)
                                          |v(Γn )|     (n − 1)t−1

where (n − 1)t−1 ≜ (n − 1)(n − 2) · · · (n − t + 1).

Proof of Lemma 7. Let R ≜ ϕ−1 (v ⋆ ) be the (random) template vertex mapped by ϕ to the
ambient anchor v ⋆ . Because ϕ is a uniform injection, R is uniform on v(Γn ), hence
                                           1     X
                           P [E(H)] =              P [E(H)|R = v] .                        (220)
                                        |v(Γn )|
                                                   v∈v(Γn )


Fix v ∈ v(Γn ). Conditional on R = v, we have ϕ(v) = v ⋆ , and the remaining k − 1 template
vertices are mapped to [n] \ {v ⋆ } uniformly without replacement. For a fixed root-preserving
template embedding ψ : H ,→ Γn with ψ(r⋆ ) = v, define the event

                         Eψ ≜ {ϕ(ψ(w)) = aw for all w ∈ v(H) \ {r⋆ }} ,                    (221)

where {aw : w ̸= r} ⊂ [n] \ {v ⋆ } is any fixed set of t − 1 distinct ambient vertices that we
want the non-root pattern vertices to land on. Conditional on R = v, the probability that
ϕ realizes Eψ is exactly
                                                              1
                                  P [E(H)|R = v] =                   ,                     (222)
                                                          (n − 1)t−1

because once ϕ(v) = v ⋆ is fixed, the remaining t − 1 pattern vertices must occupy the t − 1
distinct labels {aw } in a one-to-one fashion, and the remaining |v(Γn )| − 1 labels are uniform
without replacement.
     Crucially, for a fixed v, the events {Eψ }ψ over distinct root-preserving embeddings ψ
are disjoint: two different ψ’s require ϕ to send (at least) one different template vertex to a
specific ambient label; since ϕ is injective and the target {aw } has size t − 1, no single ϕ can
satisfy two distinct ψ’s simultaneously. Therefore
                                             X
                       P [E(H)|R = v] =            P(Eψ |R = v)                             (223)
                                           ψ: ψ(r⋆ )=v
                                                                         1
                                        = Embvert

                                                                     (n − 1)t−1

                                   1     X                           1

Lemma 9. Let Γn be a simple graph on k vertices with average degree
                        ¯ n) = 1                     2|e(Γn )|

Fix integers t ≥ 2 and d ≥ t − 1. There exists an absolute constant C ≥ 1 such that
                             1                   C(d+t)

     We begin with an intuitive sketch of the lemma before giving the formal proof. Our
goal is to bound the anchor-averaged number of rooted embeddings of any t-vertex, d-edge
connected pattern into Γn . The proof reinterprets every embedding as a growth process:
starting from the root, we reveal the pattern one vertex at a time according to a chosen
exploration scheme (a spanning tree together with an exposure order). At each step the
number of valid extensions is controlled by the edge boundary of the already-embedded set
in Γ. Averaging over relabelings makes every vertex of Γ “typical”, so that on average an
                                  ¯
s-set has boundary size about s · d(Γ). This exchangeability turns Qthe complicated boundary
terms into a clean multiplicative factor, leading to the bound t−1             ¯
                                                                      s=1 [s · d(Γ)]. The only
remaining combinatorial work is to account for the number of possible exploration schemes,
which contributes an additional factor (Ct)C(d+t) .
Proof of Lemma 9. Let Ht,d denote the family of connected rooted patterns on t vertices and
d edges (root distinguished but otherwise labeled). The proof proceeds in five steps.
1) Exploration schemes. For a connected rooted pattern H ∈ Ht,d , fix a rooted spanning
tree T ⊆ H of size t − 1, and fix an exploration order r = v1 , v2 , . . . , vt in which each vs+1 is
adjacent in T to some earlier vertex among v1 , . . . , vs . An exploration scheme S comprises:
(i) the rooted spanning tree T ; (ii) the exploration order; (iii) the choice of the extra (non-
tree) edges of H (there are d − (t − 1) of them). Let St,d be the set of all exploration schemes
over all H ∈ Ht,d .
     A crude combinatorial bound suffices: choose T (at most tt−2 rooted labeled trees by
Cayley), choose an order (at most t!), and choose the extra edges (at most                  (2t )  ≤
                                                                                         d−(t−1)
(Ct)2(d−(t−1)) ). Thus
                          |St,d | ≤ tt−2 · t! · (Ct)2(d−(t−1)) ≤ (Ct)C(d+t) .                  (249)

                                                    50
2) Partial histories and the boundary recursion. Fix a relabeling σ of v(Γn ), and
write Γσn for the relabeled graph. For an exploration scheme S ∈ St,d and an anchor v ∈ v(Γσn ),
a partial history of length s (1 ≤ s ≤ t) is a choice of an injective map sending v1 , . . . , vs to
distinct vertices of v(Γσn ) that respects the (tree) adjacencies required by S among v1 , . . . , vs .
Let Fs (σ) be the multiset of all partial histories of length s, formed by ranging over all
anchors v ∈ v(Γσn ) and all schemes S ∈ St,d .
     For f ∈ Fs (σ) write U (f ) ⊆ v(Γσn ) for the current image (so |U (f )| = s), and

                           ∂Γσn (U ) ≜ {{x, y} ∈ e(Γσn ) : x ∈ U, y ∈
                                                                    / U}                                 (250)

for the (undirected) edge boundary size of U . Each extension from s to s + 1 chooses the
next image vertex vs+1 outside U (f ) that is adjacent, in Γσn , to the specified predecessor of
vs+1 in the tree T ; consequently, for every f ∈ Fs (σ), the number of admissible choices is at
most ∂Γσn (U (f )). Summing over all f yields the recursion

6.2    Upper bounds
Single-iteration power method. Following the approach of [SW22, Sec. 4.2], to derive
an upper bound on the truncated MMSE we analyze the performance of a simple algorithm:
a single round of the power iteration method initialized with the all-ones vector, followed by
thresholding. We begin by recalling two key results from [SW22].  p Throught this section, we
let νp,q ≜ min{p, 1 − p, q, 1 − q} and we recall that λ ≜ (p − q)/ q(1 − p).
Lemma 11. [SW22, Prop. 4.1] There is a universal constant c0 ∈ (0, ∞) such that for each
k ∈ N there exists a degree-(2k + 1) polynomial τk : R → R with:

             for ℓ ∈ {0, 1},   |y − ℓ| ≤ ∆ ≤ 12 =⇒ |τk (y) − ℓ| ≤ (k + 12 )(c0 ∆)k .                (286)

Corollary 1. Fix ϵ > 0 and 0 < q < p < 1. Let Γn be any template sequence with

Proof of Corollary 1. The proof follows from Theorem 11. Indeed, (298) is satisfied under
assumption (305), while (306) implies that the term on the left-hand side of (299) is ≍ n1+2ϵ ,
while left-hand side of (299) is O(n(log n)α ) for any fixed α. Thus (299) holds for large enough
n.
     Thus, for any sequence of subgraphs Γ = Γn such that Dis(Γ) is bounded away from
one, the MSE of the algorithm proposed above matches and complements the computational
lower bound in Theorem 4. While the result above is certainly nontrivial, it does not cover
the many cases in which Dis(Γ) fails to satisfy the condition in (305). Consider the following
example.

Example 14. Split v(Γ) into two sets A and B with |A| = |B| √     = k/2: A forms a clique,
B is an independent set, and each b ∈ B is adjacent to exactly k vertices in A. In this
                                                1
construction, η(Γ) = Θ(k) and Dis(Γ) ≍ 1 ̸≤ 12    . Hence,√Corollary 1 does not apply, since
condition (305) fails, whereas our lower bound yields k ≪ n. This simple example therefore
reveals a gap.

Lemma 16. Let u ∈ v(Γ) be such that ϕ(u) = 1. Then

Proof of Lemma 16. If a path P ∈ PL is not fully contained in the planted edge-set, then
some factor Zij on that path has mean zero (conditional on ϕ), hence E[Z(P )|ϕ] = 0. If P is
fully planted, then the Zij along P are i.i.d. centered with mean (p − q), so E[Z(P )|ϕ, x =
1] = (p−q)L . The number of fully planted simple ambient paths equals the number of simple
paths of length L in Γ starting at u, namely WL (Γ; u). Summing over P ∈ PL yields the
claim.

7    Conclusion and Outlook
This work studies the problem of exact recovery of an arbitrary planted subgraph Γn in the
Erdős–Rényi random graph, in the dense regime where both the planting and noise edge
probabilities, pn and qn , are fixed independently of n. For this problem, we characterize the
statistical limits of recovery: roughly speaking, we show that if a certain graph-theoretic
quantity—termed the minimal maximum subgraph density µmin (Γn )—is below log n, then
recovery is statistically impossible, while it becomes possible (via exhaustive search) when
it is above log n. We then turn to the problem of recovery in polynomial-time. We first
propose a general algorithm that applies to arbitrary Γn and provide its statistical guarantees.
Next, we derive computational lower bounds based on the low-degree polynomial framework
recently developed in [SW22]. Finally, we discuss several extensions, including semi-random
models and weaker notions of recovery.
      We hope that our work inspires more questions than it answers. We conclude by out-
lining several avenues for future research:

1. Extending our results to settings in which the edge probabilities p and q depend on n,
   especially when they vanish or approach one (for instance, polynomially in n), entails
   technical and conceptual difficulties. Some of our techniques can be generalized to handle
   moderately sparse regimes (e.g., when p and q are as small as n−α for certain ranges of
   α), but pushing these arguments further to the general case requires new ideas.

                                              62
2. While we were able to tightly characterize the statistical limits, a gap remains between
   our lower and upper computational bounds. At present, the precise computational barrier
   for recovering an arbitrary planted subgraph is remains a mystery.
3. Our semi-random analysis focused on an adversary limited to edge deletions outside the
   planted subgraph and edge additions inside it. Considering models in which the adversary
   is allowed to add a bounded number of edges, or to perform more general perturbations,
   leads to natural questions concerning robustness and reconstructability.
4. We assume that the statistician observes the entire graph. Extending the analysis to
   settings where only part of the graph is observable—through adaptive or non-adaptive
   queries—poses an interesting and challenging problem for arbitrary planted subgraphs.
5. It is of interest, both for the detection variant and for the recovery problem studied in
   this paper, to provide additional forms of evidence for the statistical–computational gaps
   that arise. One powerful technique is through average-case reductions. For example, can
   we show that the hardness of detecting or recovering an arbitrary planted subgraph can
   be reduced from the detection or recovery of a planted clique—given that the clique is
   the “easiest” subgraph to infer?

A        Auxiliary Lemmata
A.1       Equivalence of worst-case and Bayes risks
This appendix establishes the equivalence between the worst-case and Bayesian error prob-
abilities when the prior over Γ⋆ is uniform on SΓ .
Lemma 19. Fix n ∈ N and let Γn be an arbitrary graph with no isolated vertices and at
most n vertices. Denote by SΓn the set of (labelled) copies of Γn inside the complete graph
Kn . For Γ⋆ ∈ SΓn write PΓ⋆ for the distribution GΓ⋆ (n, pn , qn ) of the observed graph G. Given

A.2     Proof of Lemma 1
Fix a step ℓ and let Γ(ℓ−1) denote the graph remaining at the beginning of that step. Recall
that for any subgraph Γ(ℓ−1) ⊊ K, we define

                                                (ℓ−1)         |K \ Γ(ℓ−1) |
                                   η(K|Γ                )≜                     .                             (370)
                                                           |v(K) \ v(Γ(ℓ−1) )|

By construction, Γ(ℓ) is a maximal subgraph that attains the maximum value

                                              η ⋆ = max η(K|Γ(ℓ−1) ).                                        (371)
                                                     Γ(ℓ−1) ⊊K


Assume, toward a contradiction, that two distinct maximisers A, B ⊊ Γ(ℓ−1) exist, i.e.,

                                       η(A|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η(B|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η ⋆ ,                                   (372)

with A ̸= B. Let C ≜ A ∪ B and define

                                              eX ≜ |X \ Γ(ℓ−1) |,                                            (373)
                                                                           (ℓ−1)
                                              vX ≜ |v(X ) \ v(Γ                     )|,                      (374)

for X ∈ {A, B, C}. Then eC = eA + eB − δ and vC = vA + vB − γ, where δ, γ ≥ 0 count
the edges and vertices already shared by A and B outside Γ(ℓ−1) . Because η(A|Γ(ℓ−1) ) =
η(B|Γ(ℓ−1) ) = η ⋆ , we have eA /vA = eB /vB = η ⋆ . Notice that by the maximiality of η ⋆ , we
have γδ ≤ η ⋆ , and consequently

                                                     eA + eB − δ   eA + eB
                          η(C|Γ(ℓ−1) ) =                         ≥         = η⋆,                             (375)
                                                     vA + vB − γ   vA + vB

with equality only if γδ = η ⋆ . Now, if the inequality is strict, namely, η(C|Γ(ℓ−1) ) > η ⋆ , then
this contradicts the optimality of η ⋆ . If equality holds, then C ⊋ A, B attains the same
maximal density, contradicting the maximality of Γ( ℓ) as A ̸= B. Thus, no two distinct
maximizers coexist.

                                                                75
A.3       Proof of Lemma 2
Recall that for any J ⊆ Γ

                                µ(Γ|J) ≜ max η(F|J),             S ⊆ Γ.                         (376)
                                           J⊊F⊆Γ

Denote the onion decomposition of Γ by

                                ∅ = Γ(0) ⊊ Γ(1) ⊊ · · · ⊊ Γ(M ) = Γ,                            (377)

and set

                                dℓ ≜ η Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) = µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ),
                                                   
                                                                                                (378)

for ℓ = 1, . . . , M . We proceed through four simple lemmas. All subgraphs are understood
to be subgraphs of Γ.

Lemma 20. If J ⊆ J′ then µ(Γ|J) ≥ µ(µ(Γ|J′ ).

Proof of Lemma 20. The feasible set {F : J ⊊ F ⊆ Γ} contains {F : J′ ⊊ F ⊆ Γ}. Maximizing
over a larger set cannot give a smaller value.

Lemma 21. Fix J. Let FJ ≜ {F : J ⊊ F ⊆ Γ, η(F|J) = µ(Γ|J)}. If F1 , F2 ∈ FJ then
F1 ∪ F2 ∈ FJ . Consequently, FJ has a unique inclusion-wise maximal element (its union).

Proof of Lemma 21. With d ≜ µ(Γ|J), set U ≜ F1 ∪ F2 , I ≜ F1 ∩ F2 . Then
                                                                                         
         |e(U|J)| − d · |v(U|J)| = |e(F1 |J)| − d · |v(F1 |J)| + |e(F2 |J)| − d · |v(F2 |J)|
                                                              
                                    − |e(I|J)| − d · |v(I|J)| ≥ 0,                              (379)

because I is feasible and hence η(I|J) ≤ d. Thus η(U|J) ≥ d, and since d is maximal, equality
holds.
                                            
Lemma 22. Recall that dℓ ≜ η Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) , for ℓ = 1, . . . , M . Then, d1 > d2 > · · · > dM .

Proof of Lemma 22. Suppose dℓ+1 ≥ dℓ . Let F maximize µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ) ), so η(F|Γ(ℓ) ) = dℓ+1 ≥ dℓ .
Then
                                         |e(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) )| + |e(F|Γ(ℓ) )|
                        η(F|Γ(ℓ−1) ) =                                     ≥ dℓ .               (380)
                                         |v(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) )| + |v(F|Γ(ℓ) )|

If the inequality is strict, this contradicts maximality of dℓ = µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ). If equality holds,
then F is also a maximizer for seed Γ(ℓ−1) ; by Lemma 21 and the onion step’s maximality,
we cannot have a strict superset F ⊋ Γ(ℓ) . This leads to a contradiction.

Lemma 23. Fix ℓ and set A ≜ Γ(ℓ−1) , B ≜ Γ(ℓ) , d ≜ dℓ = η(B|A) = µ(Γ|A). Then for every
J with A ⊆ J ⊆ B, we have µ(Γ|J) = d.



                                                   76
Proof of Lemma 23. On the one hand, by the inequality in the proof of Lemma 22 with
F = B,

                           η(B|J) ≥ η(B|A) = d                  ⇒      µ(Γ|J) ≥ d.        (381)

On the other hand, by Lemma 20, since A ⊆ J,

                                       µ(Γ|J) ≤ µ(Γ|A) = d.                               (382)

Together, µ(Γ|J) = d.
       We are now ready to prove Lemma 2, and we start by proving that T = M . Indeed,
for each ℓ, dℓ = µ(Γ|Γ(ℓ−1) ) ∈ Λ(Γ). Hence {d1 , . . . , dM } ⊆ Λ(Γ). In particular, the number
T of distinct values satisfies T ≥ M . Now, let J ⊆ Γ be arbitrary, and let ℓ be the minimal
index with J ⊆ Γ(ℓ) . Then Γ(ℓ−1) ⊆ J ⊆ Γ(ℓ) . Lemma 23 gives µ(Γ|J) = dℓ . Thus Λ(Γ) ⊆
{d1 , . . . , dM }, and so T ≤ M . Combining the above, we conclude T = M and the sets of
distinct values coincide:

                             {λ1 > · · · > λM } = {d1 > · · · > dM }.                     (383)

By Lemma 22, both sides are strictly decreasing, and thus their ℓ-th entries match:

                          λℓ = dℓ = η(Γ(ℓ) |Γ(ℓ−1) ),                 ℓ = 1, . . . , M,   (384)

which concludes the proof.
Minimal maximum subgraph density. We provide here an alternative proof for the

B      Derivation of the Maximum Likelihood Estimator
Consider the following recovery task. Pick a copy Γ⋆ ∈ SΓ . A random graph G with n
vertices is formed as follows: keep the edges of Γ with probability p, and the edges outside
Γ with probability q. We denote the ensemble of such planted graphs by GΓ⋆ (n, p, q). Given
G, the goal is to recover the graph Γ⋆ . Then, we have

C      Lower Bound Via Bayes Risk Analysis
In this appendix, we provide an alternative proof of the information-theoretic lower bounds
on recovery, derived from hypothesis testing risk analysis. The resulting bound is tight
for balanced graphs, i.e., graphs Γ with µ(Γ) = η(Γ), and for which µmin (Γ) = µ(Γ), and
with super-logarithmic maximum density, namely, µ(Γ) ≥ αn log |v(Γ)|, for some αn = Ω(1)
(which is, in fact, the interesting region). Therefore, we focus on such graphs, although

                                                   80
the method applies to arbitrary graphs as well. To that end, we begin by showing that it
suffices to consider the canonical case where pn = 1. We then provide brief preliminaries
on the detection problem of an arbitrary planted subgraph in random graphs. Next, we
propose and prove a generalized notion of the subgraph expectation threshold, which plays
an important role in some of our later proofs.
      We first observe that we can safely focus on the case where p = 1. This follows from
the fact that for any given instance of GΓn (n, pn , qn ), there exist a fixed q̃ such that recovery
is statistically easier over GΓn (n, 1, q̃n ). Indeed, let Γ̂ be any successful estimation algorithm
for the recovery of Γ over GΓn (n, pn , qn ), i.e., PGΓn (n,pn ,qn ) (Γ̂ ̸= Γ) ≤ ε, for any ε > 0. Now, let
                                                                             n            n
G ∼ G (n, 1, q /p ), and consider the random map ϕ : {0, 1}( 2 ) → {0, 1}( 2 ) , which receives
       Γn        n   n
the graph G as an input, and if (i, j) ∈ G, then we keep it with probability pn , otherwise,
if (i, j) ̸∈ G, then it remains the same. It should be clear that the random graph ϕ(G) is
distributed as follows: if (i, j) ∈ Γ, then P [[ϕ(G)]ij = 1] = pn · 1, otherwise, if (i, j) ̸∈ Γ, then
P [[ϕ(G)]ij = 1] = pn · (qn /pn ) = qn , and thus, ϕ(G) ∼ GΓn (n, pn , qn ). Furthermore, the esti-
mator Γ̂(ϕ(G)) is successful by construction. Therefore, proving the impossibility of recovery
over GΓn (n, 1, qn /pn ) implies immediately the impossibility of recovery over GΓn (n, pn , qn ).
Below, with abuse of notation, we focus on GΓn (n, 1, qn ).

C.1        Preliminaries on detection
In statistical analysis of detection problems, one of the goals is to establish a lower bound
on the optimal risk from below, thereby ruling out the possibility of successful detection. A
general recipe for this is as follows. Recall the likelihood ratio functional,

likelihood ratio test defined as,

Prop. 3]),

or weak) it suffices to upper bound the second moment of the likelihood function.

C.2     Generalized subgraph expectation threshold
A central problem in probabilistic combinatorics is to understand, for a fixed graph Γ, the
minimum edge probability p such that the random graph G(n, p) contains Γ as a subgraph
with probability at least 1/2. This threshold is commonly known as the critical threshold
for the appearance of Γ, denoted by pc (Γ). Formally, for graphs Γ and G let us denote the
number of copies of Γ in G by N (Γ, G). The critical probability of Γ is defined as,
                                                                       
                                                                      1
                  qc (Γ) ≜ min q ∈ [0, 1] PG∼G(n,q) [N (Γ, G) ≥ 1] ≥      .           (435)
                                                                      2
A long-standing conjecture by Kahn and Kalai [KK07] suggests that this critical threshold is
closely approximated—up to a logarithmic factor—by a more tractable quantity called the
subgraph expectation threshold. In [MNWSZ22], the critical probability was bounded by a
modified subgraph expectation threshold, defined as follows,
                                                                             
                                                        N (H, Γ)
        q̃E (Γ) ≜ min q ∈ [0, 1] EG∼G(n,q) [N (H, G)] ≥          for all H ⊆ Γ ,      (436)
                                                           2
where only subgraphs H ⊆ Γ with no isolated vertices are considered.
Theorem 14. [MNWSZ22, Theorem 1] There exists a universal constant C such that for
any graph Γ,
                               q̃E (Γ) ≤ qc (Γ) ≤ C · q̃E · log |e(Γ)|.                 (437)
     We would like to generalize the above notions to the scenario of the appearance of L ∈ N
distinct copies. Specifically, define the critical probability as,

                      (L)

                              1

     The proof of Theorem 15 relies on a powerful probabilistic tool known as the spread
lemma, which has been instrumental in several recent breakthroughs. To describe the lemma
in our context, consider a probability distribution π supported on subgraphs of the complete
graph Kn . Let α > 1. We say that the distribution π is α-spread if, for every (non-empty)
subgraph H ⊆ Kn ,
                                     π(H ⊆ Γ) ≤ α−|e(H)| ,                             (445)
where Γ ∼ π. The following result—adapted from Theorem 1.6 in [FKNP21]—gives a
threshold condition under which a random graph drawn from G(n, p) is likely to intersect a
family of such subgraphs.

Lemma 25 (Spread lemma [MNWSZ22, Lemma 2]). Fix integers k ≥ 1 and M ≥ 1. Let
GM ≜ {G1 , . . . , GM } ⊆ Kn satisfy |e(Gi )| ≤ k for every i. Let π be the uniform measure on
GM , and assume the α-spread condition in (445). Then, there is an absolute constant C > 0
such that if q > C logα k , then a sample from G(n, q) contains one of the Gi ’s with probability
at least 1/2.

      We now propose the following generalization of the above spread lemma.

Theorem 16. Fix L ≥ 1. Let Γ = (Γn ) be a sequence of graphs such that ω(1) ≤ |v(Γ)| ≤ n

              (1 + ε)µ(Γn ) · [log log(L|e(Γn )|) + log(CL)] + log |v(Γn )| ≤ log n,        (460)

D      Almost-exact recovery
In this appendix, we prove Theorems 9. To that end, we start with some preliminaries.

D.1     Preliminaries and auxiliary results
Equivalent notions of recovery. Recall that an estimator Γ̂ almost-exactly recover Γ⋆
if, as n → ∞, dH (Γ̂, Γ⋆ )/|e(Γ)| → 0 in probability. We have the following result.
Lemma 28. An estimator Γ̂ almost-exactly recover Γ⋆ if and only if EdH (Γ̂, Γ⋆ )/|e(Γ)| → 0.
Proof of Lemma 28. The forward implication follows immediately, since L1 -convergence en-
tails convergence in probability [Fol99, Proposition 6.14]. For the other direction, assume
there is Γ̂ with
                                            dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) P
                                                         −
                                                         → 0,                                  (504)
                                                m
where m ≜ |e(Γ)|. Let Πm (Γ̂) be any m-sparse vector minimizing Hamming distance to Γ̂,
i.e., delete or add ones to reach size m). Then, by triangle inequality,
                     dH (Γ⋆ , Πm (Γ̂)) ≤ dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) + dH (Γ̂, Πm (Γ̂))                       (505)
                                       = dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) + |Γ̂| − m                               (506)
                                       = dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) + |supp(Γ̂)| − |supp(Γ⋆ )|               (507)
                                       ≤ 2 · dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂).                                     (508)

Furthermore, |Γ⋆ | = |Πm (Γ̂)| = m gives dH (Γ⋆ , Πm (Γ̂)) ≤ 2m. Hence
                                                                  !
                            dH (Γ⋆ , Πm (Γ̂))      dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂)
                                              ≤2                ∧1 ,                           (509)
                                   m                   m

                                                                                   R1
with probability one. Now, for any nonnegative X, we have X ∧ 1 =                  0

                      "                   #      "                  !#
                        dH (Γ⋆ , Πm (Γ̂))           dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂)
                    E                       ≤ 2E                 ∧1                            (510)

Because dH (Γm ,Γ̂) −
                    → 0, for every fixed t > 0 we have P(dH (Γ⋆ , Γ̂) > mt) → 0; the integrand is

                                  Z 1                     !

which implies that E[dH (Γ, Πm (Γ̂))]/m → 0. Thus, the estimator Γ̃ ≜ Πm (Γ̂) satisfies
E[dH (Γ⋆ , Πm (Γ̂))/m] → 0, as required.

D.3     Lower bound in sub-logarithmic density regime
As it turns out, the proof technique in the previous subsection is not strong enough to
capture the correct behavior in the sub-logarithmic density regime. Specifically, Theorem 17
shows that, in this regime, recovery is impossible if (527) holds. However, as we show next,
recovery is in fact always impossible in this regime.
Theorem 18. Assume that q ∈ (0, 1) is fixed and that |v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )| = o(n). If

                                           log |v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )|
                                                                     
                      ⋆ (ℓLB ),⋆
                   µ(Γ |Γ        )=o                                    ,                           (555)
                                         log log |v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )|
then almost-exact recovery is impossible.
     To prove Theorem 18, we need a generalization of the subgraph expectation threshold
that was already discussed in Appendix D.3.1. Specifically, the original subgraph expectation
threshold in Theorem 14 analyzes the threshold for the appearance of any possible copy of
the planted subgraph Γ (in the complete graph Kn ) within G ∼ G(n, q). For our purposes,
however, some copies are precluded. Let us explain this in detail.
     In a nutshell, recall that we are in the scenario where the recovery problem is supplied
with Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ and tasked with finding Γ⋆ . Therefore, the admissible Γ⋆ ’s are only those that
extend Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , namely, the copies contained in M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ). We resolve this by deriving
a generalization of the subgraph expectation threshold that accounts for the appearance of
a constrained set of subgraph copies. The details are provided in Appendix D.3.1, where we
also establish the following key result.
Lemma 30. Let Γ = (Γn ) be a sequence of graphs such that ω(1) ≤ |v(Γ)| ≤ n, and
(555) holds. Then, for any fixed q, a sample from G(n, q) contains an isomorphic copy of
Γ ∈ M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ), with probability at least 1/2.
     Let us now prove Theorem 18, and then move forward to the proof of Lemma 30.

                                                             95
Proof of Theorem 18. We prove that
                            h                                     i 1
                                        (ℓ)      ⋆            ⋆
                                                   
                 inf sup PΓ⋆ dH T̂ (G, Γ ), Tℓ (Γ ) ≥ |e(Tℓ (Γ ))| ≥ ,                              (556)
                  T̂ Γ⋆ ∈SΓ                                         4

where we use PΓ⋆ to emphasize that G ∼ GΓ⋆ (n, 1, q), with Γ⋆ being the underlying planted
subgraph. Proving (556) then readily implies that almost-exact recovery is impossible. Recall
that by Lemma 30, for all sufficiently large n, and since |v(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )| ≤ |v(Γ)| = o(n), under
the condition in (555), a typical sample from G(n − |v(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ )|, q) contains an isomorphic
copy of Γ⋆ ∈ M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ), with probability at least 1/2. Denote this copy by Γ′ , and
note that by construction, both Γ⋆ and Γ′ are extensions of the same subgraph Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ,
and furthermore, v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) ∩ v(Γ′ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) = ∅, namely, their tails are vertex-disjoint.
Accordingly, define the event

   FΓ⋆ ≜ ∃ Γ′ ∈ M(Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ , Γ⋆ ) with v(Γ⋆ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) ∩ v(Γ′ \ Γ(ℓLB ),⋆ ) = ∅, e(Γ′ ) ⊆ e(G) .

D.3.1    Proof of Lemma 30
In this subsection we prove Lemma 30. To that end, let us introduce a few definitions and
notations. Fix a graph Γ and a subgraph J ⊊ Γ. Recall that M(J, Γ) is the set of copies of
Γ in Kn that contain J. Let

                                                1 {Γ′ ∈ G} ,

bility of Γ w.r.t. to M is defined as

We are now in a position to prove Theorem 19.
Proof of Theorem 19. Let πΓ = Unif(M(J, Γ)) denotes the uniform measure over all copies
of a fixed graph Γ within Kn that contain J, and let Γ′ ∼ πΓ be a random sample from this
distribution. Now, consider a subgraph H such that J ⊆ H ⊂ Γ. Let πH = Unif(M(J, H))
denotes the uniform measure over all copies of H within Kn that contain J, and let H′ ∼ πH .
For any fixed instances H0 , Γ0 ⊆ Kn , copies of H and Γ, respectively, we can compute the
inclusion probability in two equivalent ways:
                                                      |M(H, Γ)|    N (H, Γ)

Since the bound holds uniformly over all subgraphs H0 , we conclude that πΓ is α-spread with
α = 1/[2q̃E (J; Γ)]. An application of Lemma 26 with k = |e(Γ \ J)| then completes the proof
of Theorem 19.


    Next, we prove the following result.
Theorem 20. Let Γ = (Γn ) and J = (Jn ) be sequences of graphs such that ω(1) ≤ |v(Γ)| ≤ n,
J ⊆ Γ, and
       (1 + ε)µ(Γn |Jn ) · [log log |e(Γn \ J)| + log C] + log |v(Γn ) \ v(Jn )| ≤ log n,             (588)
for some ε > 0 and C > 0. Then, for any fixed q, a sample from G(n, q) contains an
isomorphic copy of Γ′ ∈ M(J, Γ), with probability at least 1/2.
Proof of Theorem 20. From the definition of qc (J; Γ) in (579), our goal is to understand for
which Γ’s we have qc (J; Γ) ≤ q, for any fixed q ∈ (0, 1]. By Theorem 19, it is sufficient to
                              q
show that q̃E (J; Γ) ≤ C log(|e(Γ\J)|) for any fixed q, which holds if and only if

equals πΓ [H ⊆ Γ], where πΓ = Unif(M(J, Γ)) denotes the uniform measure over all copies of
a fixed graph Γ within Kn that contain J. Thus, (591) holds if,

           log πΓ [H ⊆ Γ] − log 2 − |e(H \ J)| (log q − log C − log log |e(Γ \ J)|) ≤ 0.      (592)

By the assumptions that |v(Γ)| = ω(1), and that Γ has no isolated vertices, for any ε and
for sufficiently large n, the above holds if,

           log πΓ [H ⊆ Γ] + |e(H \ J)| log C + (1 + ε)|e(H \ J)| log log |e(Γ \ J)| ≤ 0.      (593)

  log πΓ [H ⊆ Γ] + |e(H \ J)| log C + (1 + ε)|e(H \ J)| log log |e(Γ \ J)|                    (594)
